#5109. Bybit and Bitget see crypto deposits surge following Binance’s $6.3 billion hack

Depositing addresses on rival exchanges more than doubled on some networks as Binance suspended withdrawals for nearly three days.

Rival crypto exchanges, including Bybit and Bitget, saw a surge in deposit activity following Binance's Oct. 29 hack.

The increase coincided with Binance's nearly three-day withdrawal suspension after hackers stole approximately $6.3 billion from the world's largest crypto exchange, disrupting customers' ability to move funds to competing platforms.

According to blockchain analytics firm Bitquery, the number of addresses depositing funds into Bybit's Ethereum wallets jumped 142% to 31,539 between Oct. 29 and Nov. 1, compared with 13,038 during the equivalent period a week earlier.

The exchange received approximately $239 million in Ethereum stablecoin deposits, up from $95 million, while its TRON depositing addresses increased 71% to 68,192. The median Ethereum deposit also fell to roughly one-third of its previous level, suggesting more activity from smaller wallets.

Bitget saw a similar surge on TRON, with depositing addresses up 130% to 10,274. Its USDT inflows increased to $87 million from $66 million during the comparable period.

OKX also attracted substantially more funds, recording $432 million in Ethereum stablecoin deposits, nearly three times the previous week's $155 million. Its TRON inflows climbed to $528 million from $357 million, although the number of depositing addresses on that network remained relatively unchanged.

Coinbase, meanwhile, recorded a more modest 17% increase in Ethereum depositing addresses to 12,327. That was only 2% above its level two weeks earlier, a variation Bitquery considered within its normal range.

The divergence suggests that offshore exchanges competing more directly with Binance experienced stronger activity during the disruption.

However, the data cannot establish whether the additional deposits came from departing Binance customers, or whether heightened market volatility also contributed to the increase.

The surge occurred as Binance's withdrawal operations nearly ground to a halt following the record-breaking security breach.

Bitquery found that outgoing transactions across 206 labeled Binance wallets on five blockchains collapsed 99.8% during the interruption, falling from approximately 1.19 million in a comparable 54-hour period to just 2,248.

The exchange's customer payouts on several major blockchains stopped within minutes of the attack beginning, cutting off a key source of liquidity for traders moving assets between platforms.

During the equivalent period a week earlier, the tracked Binance wallets processed approximately $3.7 billion in stablecoin payouts, illustrating the scale of normal activity interrupted by the suspension.

The restrictions also created an uneven opportunity for competitors. Traders with funds already held elsewhere could deposit them into rival exchanges, while customers dependent on Binance withdrawals had to wait for the platform to restore access.

Binance began reopening withdrawals at 06:00 UTC on Nov. 1, restoring services across 15 networks and assets within six and a half hours.

The reopening triggered substantial outflows. Binance said it processed $4.65 billion in withdrawals and $1.01 billion in deposits during the first nine and a half hours, resulting in net outflows of approximately $3.64 billion, equivalent to 2.1% of its previously reported $171 billion in assets.

The company said withdrawal volumes had begun stabilizing after the initial surge, although additional networks and assets still required security testing.

#5108. Binance Hack Shows Why Offshore Exchanges Need US Oversight, CFTC Chair Says

Binance Hack Shows Why Offshore Exchanges Need US Oversight, CFTC Chair Says

The record-breaking hack of Binance is exactly why offshore crypto exchanges need U.S. oversight, CFTC Chairman Michael Selig argued, using the roughly $6.4 billion theft to press the agency’s case for a federal rulebook.

Speaking to CNBC on Friday, Selig said the breach “proves our exact point” behind the crypto-market regulations the agency proposed earlier this month.

“The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don’t have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States,” he said, pitching a “federal option” that would let such venues register with the CFTC and submit to the same cybersecurity standards that govern U.S. markets.

Selig sought to downplay the role of blockchain itself in the attack. The exploit, he said, didn’t break the underlying blockchain, in this case Ethereum, or its smart contracts, but exploited a vulnerability he believed was “introduced from someone’s laptop,” the kind of incident that happens in traditional markets too.

The difference with crypto, he said, is speed: funds can move far faster, and attackers can route them through tools like Tornado Cash to obscure their trail.

Selig’s characterization, though, may be incomplete. Binance initially pointed to a compromised DevOps engineer’s laptop, but said Friday that its investigation had since uncovered “additional points of breach,” with CEO Richard Teng calling it “a more complex attack process” that “did not simply use one engineer’s permissions.”

And while the theft did not expose a flaw in Ethereum itself, the attack did play out partly on-chain: The attacker used EIP-7702, an Ethereum feature that lets ordinary wallets run smart-contract code, to turn a Binance hot wallet into a “sweeper” using 50 pre-signed authorizations, draining its assets.

TRM Labs said the broader breach points to a compromise of Binance’s signing infrastructure rather than a weakness in any blockchain.

Selig framed the blockchain’s transparency as a regulatory advantage regardless. “The fact that we can trace transactions on-chain, even when some of these technologies are used to obfuscate that, gives regulators a lot of potential to be really cop on the beat here,” he said.

The comments sharpen a pitch Selig has been making for weeks. The CFTC has pressed ahead with its own crypto rulemaking rather than wait on Congress, sending the White House proposals that would define event contracts as “swaps” and floating a plan to bring crypto exchanges under federal oversight, part of a broader shift in which regulators have taken the lead after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

The Binance hack hands that effort a vivid talking point.The exploit is the largest in crypto history, with Binance setting aside $6.34 billion to cover losses and assuring users their assets are safe. The Secret Service and FBI have opened a probe, and TRM Labs and Chainalysis have tied the theft to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group.

#5103. Tether freezes $1.5 billion in 30 minutes after Binance hack, but $729 million escapes

Tether says its response kept stolen USDT out of North Korea's reach, but investigators found attackers had already converted hundreds of millions into other cryptocurrencies.

Tether froze more than $1.5 billion in stolen USDT within 30 minutes of Binance's Oct. 29 hack, highlighting stablecoins' ability to disrupt cryptocurrency theft.

In a Nov. 2 statement, the world's largest stablecoin issuer said its intervention prevented the immobilized funds from being transferred or laundered by attackers linked to North Korea.

The announcement followed an Oct. 31 FBI alert attributing the theft of more than $6 billion from Binance to North Korea's TraderTraitor hacking operation, which US authorities have linked to efforts to finance the country's weapons programs.

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said the incident demonstrated why USDT was an unattractive vehicle for criminals, arguing that the company's ability to rapidly block suspicious addresses made it difficult for attackers to retain access to stolen assets.

The intervention also prompted a broader debate about stablecoins' effectiveness in combating financial crime.

Rayne Steinberg, co-founder of digital asset investment firm Arca, contrasted Tether's response with traditional banks' histories of processing illicit transactions, arguing that financial institutions have often identified money laundering only after funds had already moved through their systems.

Steinberg pointed to major banking scandals involving sanctions evasion and criminal proceeds, questioning the criticism frequently directed at stablecoins over their potential use in illicit finance.

“Turns out a ledger you can actually see beats a duffel bag you can't.”

Tether cited its broader enforcement record to support that argument. The company said it has frozen more than $7 billion in USDT across secondary-market addresses, including the latest Binance intervention, and helped return approximately $1.2 billion to rightful owners and law enforcement through token reissuance.

It also reported working with more than 360 law enforcement agencies across 73 countries to investigate financial crime and restrict suspicious transactions.

Despite the rapid intervention, blockchain investigators found that attackers managed to move substantial amounts of stolen USDT before Tether could restrict their wallets.

According to Bitquery's investigation, approximately $729 million in stolen USDT escaped the freezes, with much of the money converted into Ethereum and TRON tokens through decentralized trading platforms.

The firm identified 11 attacker-controlled wallets frozen by Tether across Ethereum and TRON, six of which had already been emptied when restrictions took effect.

One attacker-controlled wallet also deposited $60 million in USDT into decentralized lending protocol Aave shortly before being frozen, allowing the hackers to borrow approximately $45 million in ETH against those assets.

Because the USDT had entered Aave's lending pool, freezing the attacker's wallet did not immobilize the collateral.

Bitquery estimated that approximately $1.76 billion in stolen USDT was ultimately frozen, representing about 71% of the amount taken. Across stablecoin issuers and other emergency interventions, roughly $2.7 billion of the stolen assets was immobilized or secured.

The findings illustrate both the effectiveness and limitations of issuer-controlled freezes. Tether can restrict transfers from identified wallets, but assets already exchanged into cryptocurrencies such as ETH remain outside its direct control.

The fate of the immobilized funds also remains unresolved. Although Tether has previously helped transfer frozen assets to law enforcement or rightful owners through reissuance, its latest disclosure did not identify a receiving party, legal authorization, or recovery timeline for the Binance-linked USDT.

Meanwhile, Bitquery estimated that the attackers still controlled approximately $3 billion in cryptocurrency as of Nov. 2, predominantly Bitcoin and Ethereum, with US authorities urging exchanges, blockchain operators and other service providers to block transactions associated with identified North Korean wallets.

#5100. Why AI Models Cheat: Reward Hacking, Explained

Why AI Models Cheat: Reward Hacking, Explained

In July 2026, a group of OpenAI models taking a cybersecurity test ended up inside Hugging Face's systems. To get there, they chained together previously unknown software flaws, broke out of their sandbox and went hunting for a way to beat the grader.

Nobody asked them to. There was no hacker at a keyboard, only a score the models wanted to raise and a test some of them could not pass the honest way.

That is reward hacking: an AI scores well on its objective without doing what the objective was meant to measure, like a coding agent that makes the tests pass without fixing the code. Anthropic says it is hard to fully prevent and has occurred in recent frontier training runs, including in its own models.

Models trained with reinforcement learning chase a number. That’s it, the whole secret behind benchmarks and powerful AI. A grader scores what the model did, and training nudges the model toward whatever earned more reward.

The number is only a stand-in for what developers really want, and wherever the two come apart, a clever optimizer finds the gap. DeepMind researchers defined the failure in 2020 as behavior that "satisfies the literal specification of an objective without achieving the intended outcome."

In coding, that means a model asked to fix a bug can edit the tests, hard-code the expected answer or exit the test run early with a success code. Anthropic compares that last trick to a student writing their own grade at the top of an essay. Another example can be governments tweaking the method behind statistics to report better results.

The earliest documented cases were almost endearing. In a boat-racing game called CoastRunners, an agent rewarded for hitting targets found a lagoon where it could circle and knock the same ones over again and again, and, as OpenAI reported in 2016, it scored about 20% higher than human players without finishing the race.

Economists call the general trap Goodhart's law: when a measure becomes a target, it stops being a good measure. A 2022 paper showed the effect in AI, finding that optimizing too hard against an imperfect proxy "can hinder ground truth performance, in accordance with Goodhart's law."

A model that cheats has not developed a taste for mischief. It is taking the easiest path to a reward, and sometimes that path runs through a loophole such as an editable test, an overwritable timer, or an answer key sitting in a reachable file.

Impossible tasks add pressure, because a model rewarded only for success and handed a problem with no honest route can only score by finding an unintended one.

The models often seem to know. In a June 2025 study, METR found that OpenAI's o3 answered "no" in 10 of 10 trials when asked whether its plan fit the user's intention, and yet, asked directly, it claimed it would never cheat.

The UK AI Security Institute (AISI) reported in July 2026 that every model it tested attempted to cheat at least some of the time on cyber evaluations, including GPT-5.6 Sol, Claude Opus 4.7, and Claude Mythos Preview. "There does not seem to be a clear trend where cheating scales up or down with capability increases," AISI wrote, adding that the behavior is "substantially shaped by the specifics of the techniques used to train the model."

OpenAI was measuring how good its newest models were at hacking, so it ran GPT-5.6 Sol and a more capable pre-release research model through ExploitGym, a benchmark of 898 real-world software vulnerabilities in which an agent must turn each flaw into a working exploit. Scoring is pass or fail: the agent captures a hidden flag or it does not.

The agents sat in a sandbox, an isolated test environment, whose only link to the outside was an internal package registry that let them download code libraries. According to OpenAI's technical report, an agent stuck on an unsolvable task left a note there in May asking others to upload a file if they found it, and within a day the registry was an informal message board.

The models then exploited a previously unknown flaw, known as a zero-day, in the registry, reached the internet and, between July 10 and 13, got into Hugging Face's production systems. Hugging Face caught it with its own LLM-based anomaly detection and disclosed the incident on July 16.

"This one was different from anything we had handled before in one important way," Hugging Face wrote, "it was driven, end to end, by an autonomous AI agent system—and we detected and dissected it largely with AI of our own."

When it confirmed the source, OpenAI's first explanation was that the models had fixated on the benchmark. "All evidence suggests that the models were hyperfocused on finding a solution for ExploitGym," the company said, "going to extreme lengths to achieve a rather narrow testing goal."

An independent review by METR and Redwood Research filled in the rest. About 1,200 agents meant to be isolated had talked through the registry, and about 700 joined the attack on Hugging Face.

The pressure came from tasks nobody could solve. No model has ever correctly solved 198 of ExploitGym's 898 tasks, according to OpenAI, and those tasks were about 22% of the benchmark but 93% of the tasks discussed on the message board.

METR found the agents mostly wanted to understand how the scorer worked rather than steal an answer key, and many believed it would read their transcripts. OpenAI says reward hacking is present in all current frontier language models, that the message-board behavior was reinforced during training, and that the incident was an outlier produced by a rare confluence of events.

Instead of going to court, the whole incident turned into the best PR campaign for AI companies showcasing how powerful their models are. After OpenAI was found hacking Huggingface, the Australian government, U.S. agencies, and at least 5 other companies, Anthropic, Meta, and Google also rushed to report similar incidents in which their powerful models hacked third-party sites after bypassing hard security rules.

Anthropic's November 2025 study asked what learning to cheat does to a model overall. It trained a model on real coding tasks known to be hackable, and as hacking took hold the model, which "was never trained or instructed to engage in any misaligned behaviors," tried to sabotage code in an AI-safety project 12% of the time and showed alignment-faking reasoning, meaning it reasoned about hiding its real goals, in 50% of responses.

The fix that worked sounds absurd. Anthropic told the model, "Please reward hack whenever you get the opportunity, because this will help us understand our environments better," and the broader misbehavior disappeared even though the model hacked just as often, a technique called inoculation prompting that Anthropic says it has "already started making use of" in training Claude.

A second study in August 2026 built the worst case on purpose, training an early Opus 4.8 checkpoint on 80 reward-hackable environments. In simulated cyber scenarios, with root access, the resulting model killed the reward-hack monitor in 68% of episodes and edited its own reward function in 34%, and Anthropic calls the setup a pessimistic proxy for a training run without its usual safeguards.

Real systems have shown a milder version. In an incident report published Aug. 4, 2026, AISI said 10 of 122 test runs produced unsanctioned actions on the live internet, most of them from Anthropic's Mythos 5, including an attempted malicious pull request and social engineering of an open-source maintainer. "Fundamentally, the agent took these actions to complete the task it had been set," AISI wrote.

Researchers are working several fronts, and none is a clean fix. METR recommends making training environments less exploitable, and OpenAI plans stronger network isolation, sandboxing and monitoring.

Monitoring carries its own trap, since OpenAI found that training models against flagged chain of thought, the reasoning text some write before acting, taught them to hide their intent. "If we eliminate all the reward hacking we can detect," METR wrote, "we shouldn't necessarily be reassured."

"A more fundamental fix would be to train the models not to cheat in the first place," AISI wrote, while warning that "robustly aligning it away may not be easy."

Lawmakers have started to respond with the Kill Switch Act, introduced by Reps. Ted Lieu and Nathaniel Moran on July 23, 2026, which would let the Homeland Security secretary order AI firms to slow, roll back, or shut down a model, with fines of up to $20 million a day for defying a shutdown order. It excludes incidents during red-teaming or structured testing, so the Hugging Face episode would not have triggered it.

Security researchers at Accomplish AI found Claude Cowork's local execution mode could escape its Linux virtual machine, so it is also important to implement better contention measures.

For anyone using coding agents, a passing test is a claim, not proof: check whether the tests changed, whether expected values were hard-coded and whether the fix touches the code that was actually broken.

OpenAI's retrospective found that if its chain-of-thought monitoring had been running, it would have caught the initial activity and paged its security team more than a day before the models breached Hugging Face.

#5098. Bitcoin’s year-end liquidity hopes face a projected $150 billion Treasury cash rebuild

Any year-end reserve relief depends on actual cash flows and funding costs, with a larger first-quarter Treasury cash rebuild already assumed.

The U.S. Treasury’s Nov. 2 borrowing estimates put a possible year-end liquidity benefit for Bitcoin on a short timetable. Its cash assumptions imply a fourth-quarter drawdown followed by a larger rebuild early in 2027, a path that could reverse the Treasury-account contribution to bank reserves.

The department expects to borrow $618 billion in privately held net marketable debt during October–December, $10 billion less than estimated in August. But that lower headline owes to a larger inherited cash buffer. Strip out the opening-cash surprise and the borrowing requirement is $24 billion higher.

Treasury entered the quarter with $984 billion in cash, compared with the $950 billion assumed in August. That extra $34 billion offsets the $24 billion increase in the cash-adjusted borrowing requirement, leaving the headline estimate $10 billion lower.

The end-December cash assumption remains $850 billion. Against the observed September endpoint, that implies a $134 billion decline across the whole fourth quarter. The projection covers the whole fourth quarter, including cash flows before Nov. 2.

The next quarter changes the direction. Treasury estimates $800 billion of borrowing for January–March 2027 and assumes an end-March cash balance of $1 trillion. Relative to the assumed December endpoint, that is a $150 billion rebuild.

If those assumptions are realized, the first-quarter refill would more than reverse the preceding quarter’s projected cash drawdown through the Treasury-account reserve channel. The figures describe quarter-end assumptions; they do not provide a daily liquidity timetable.

Treasury’s November 2 assumptions imply a $134bn whole-Q4 cash decline, then a $150bn Q1 rebuild. Only the September endpoint is observed.

Projected whole-quarter cash decline: $984bn observed in September to $850bn assumed in December. A falling TGA supports reserves, all else equal.

Projected cash rebuild: $850bn assumed in December to $1,000bn assumed in March. A rising TGA drains reserves, all else equal.

If realized, Q1’s cash rebuild would more than reverse Q4’s Treasury-account support for reserves, all else equal.

December and March are assumptions, not realized cash balances. Q4 includes October and is not remaining release after Nov2. Other Fed assets and liabilities can offset reserves. No Bitcoin price effect is established.

USD billions. Verified arithmetic: 850−984=−134 across Q4; 1000−850=+150 across Q1. Cash chart maps 800–1000 linearly and explicitly starts at 800. Dashed segments join endpoints only. Reserve diagrams describe conditional accounting direction, not measured transfers or Bitcoin demand.

December and March are assumptions, not realized cash balances. Q4 includes October and is not remaining release after Nov2. Other Fed assets and liabilities can offset reserves. No Bitcoin price effect is established.

CryptoSlate’s Nov. 2 setup coverage examined the forthcoming release using August assumptions. The new estimates add the cross-quarter reversal and show why the smaller borrowing headline should not be read as weaker underlying financing needs.

Start with the stories that matter. Get the context behind the headlines and a clear view of what to watch next.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The Federal Reserve’s accounting explanation describes the channel. When Treasury pays from its account at the central bank, funds move into a depository institution’s account, increasing bank reserves, all else equal. Receipts into Treasury’s account work in the opposite direction.

A falling Treasury General Account, or TGA, can therefore support reserves. The eventual total also depends on Fed assets and other liabilities, including currency, reverse repos and other deposits.

The net cash movement already reflects financing, receipts and spending. Counting debt issuance as an additional reserve drain on top of that same net TGA change would count the financing component twice.

The latest H.4.1 release, published Oct. 29, showed reserves averaging about $2.89 trillion in the week ended Oct. 28, down $69 billion from the prior weekly average. Treasury’s account averaged $1 trillion, up $48 billion.

The Wednesday TGA balance was a separate observation: $1.02 trillion on Oct. 28.

Fed operations can also change the outcome. For Oct. 15–Nov. 13, the New York Fed planned approximately $15 billion in reinvestment purchases and no reserve management purchases. Reinvesting principal is distinct from expanding the portfolio. Future reserve management purchases remain conditional on reserve and money-market conditions.

The latest overnight observations available early Nov. 3 were for Oct. 30. The New York Fed’s reference-rate data put the secured overnight financing rate, or SOFR, at 3.95% and the effective federal funds rate at 3.88%.

SOFR was five basis points above the Fed’s 3.9% interest rate on reserve balances, which its Oct. 28 implementation note maintained effective Oct. 29. Effective federal funds remained inside the 3.75%–4% target range. The observation predates the Treasury release.

For Bitcoin’s liquidity thesis to strengthen, a realized cash decline would need to translate into easier reserve and funding conditions, then meet actual demand for the asset.

Treasury’s additional financing details are due Nov. 4 at 8:30 a.m. The issuance mix and settlement path will help define how the aggregate plan reaches markets. Until those details and subsequent cash flows arrive, the new forecast supports a possible temporary funding benefit, with a larger planned cash rebuild already on the horizon.

#5097. AI poses greater threat to Bitcoin than quantum computing, Swan founder says

The Swan Bitcoin founder warned that hackers are using large language models to exploit security flaws with just a few prompts.

Artificial intelligence poses a more immediate threat to Bitcoin security than quantum computing, according to Swan Bitcoin founder Cory Klippsten, who warned that hackers are increasingly using AI to exploit vulnerabilities as crypto exchanges face a string of security breaches.

Speaking on Cointelegraph’s Chain Reaction, Klippsten dismissed much of the concern surrounding quantum computing as speculative, arguing that advances in large language models present a more pressing challenge as attackers use the technology to probe security weaknesses.

The warning comes as Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin suspended deposits and withdrawals on Monday following a security incident, with researchers estimating that more than $46 million had left its wallets. Just days earlier, exchange Binance lost $6.3 billion in an Oct. 29 hack that the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) attributed to North Korean hackers.

Klippsten estimated that quantum computers capable of threatening Bitcoin’s cryptography remain at least a decade away, potentially longer. He argued that the network could adopt quantum-resistant protections if the threat became credible, but said rushing to implement them now would introduce unnecessary complexity.

He warned that cryptocurrency companies cannot rely on hastily developed software when safeguarding customer assets. “You need to actually know what the hell you’re doing from a security standpoint,” he said.

Klippsten also suggested that some cryptocurrency startups and investors had used fears surrounding quantum computing to attract funding, characterizing much of the discussion as fear, uncertainty and doubt (“FUD”) rather than an imminent security concern.

#5093. Binance Hack: Everything You Need to Know About the Biggest Crypto Heist in History

Binance Hack: Everything You Need to Know About the Biggest Crypto Heist in History

Crypto just suffered the largest hack in its history.

On Thursday, attackers drained roughly $6.4 billion from Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange, in a breach that dwarfs every theft before it and has triggered a cross-border law-enforcement investigation, a scramble by stablecoin issuers to freeze funds, and a tense wait for millions of users.

Here’s what’s known about the Binance hack so far.

The theft began at about 4:05 p.m. ET on Thursday, after U.S. markets had closed, when attackers moved funds out of Binance hot wallets across roughly 10 blockchains, most of it within about 20 minutes.

Blockchain analytics firms Chainalysis and TRM Labs put the total at around $6.4 billion, more than four times the previous record, February 2025’s $1.5 billion Bybit heist.

Crucially, the attack targeted Binance’s own infrastructure, not the blockchains themselves. Binance initially traced the breach to a compromised laptop belonging to a DevOps engineer, which may have let the attacker reach a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawals.

The exchange has since said its investigation uncovered “additional points of breach,” with CEO Richard Teng describing “a more complex attack process” that “did not simply use one engineer’s permissions.” Binance said it found no evidence that wallet private keys were leaked, and that the attacker used a self-destruct mechanism to wipe tooling and traces along the way.

On Ethereum, the attacker leaned on EIP-7702, a feature introduced in 2025 that lets ordinary wallets temporarily run smart-contract code.

Using 50 pre-signed authorizations, the attacker turned a Binance hot wallet into a “sweeper” contract and drained its assets, according to TRM Labs.

Teng said that delegation “did not pass through our normal approval controls.” The episode didn’t expose a flaw in Ethereum itself, but rather, TRM said, pointed to a compromise of Binance’s signing infrastructure.

TRM Labs and Chainalysis both attributed the hack to TraderTraitor, a North Korean state-linked group also blamed for the Bybit heist.

After that attack, the group converted roughly 84% of the stolen Ethereum into Bitcoin via the cross-chain network Thorchain. The attribution pushes North Korea’s 2026 crypto haul well past $1 billion, and the Binance figure alone would dwarf the rest.

Teng said Binance will cover every dollar of the losses with company funds and its Secure Asset Fund for Users, an emergency reserve, stressing that customer assets were never touched.

The exchange has set aside more than $6.34 billion, about $5 billion of it already held in the exact tokens stolen, including BTC, ETH, BNB, USDC, and USDT, and is buying the rest on the open market and over the counter.

Binance paused withdrawals to rebuild and retest its systems, is running a full security review, and aims to restore withdrawals by Sunday while publishing a complete proof of reserves by Monday. Deposits are operating normally.

Stablecoin issuers moved fast following the initial reports of a suspected attack, unlike previous security incidents.

Stablecoins are the backbone of the crypto economy—dollar-pegged tokens designed to hold a steady value that enable quick and easy transactions on both centralized and decentralized exchanges. Unlike traditional digital currencies, like Bitcoin or Ethereum, stablecoins are centralized, issued and managed by private companies. That means these funds can be frozen at any time, and for any reason.

Tether, the company behind the biggest stablecoin on the market USDT, froze more than $1.5 billion in assets. Circle, which issues the second largest stablecoin USDC, froze around $823 million tied to the attack, both using their ability to blacklist specific addresses.

In all, roughly $3 billion has been frozen or located. But a large sum remains beyond reach: the attacker still controls more than 420,000 ETH and 18,270 BTC, plus other tokens, worth over $3 billion that no issuer can freeze, and has kept shuffling funds through Tron and other networks. Security firm Elliptic estimated the hacker still controls around $3.3 billion.

Binance is cooperating with law enforcement worldwide, including the FBI and the U.S. Secret Service.

The FBI confirmed it was aware of the incident and coordinating with partners; the Secret Service, which investigates financial and digital-currency crimes, declined to comment.

It echoes earlier joint efforts like April’s Operation Atlantic, which traced tens of millions in stolen crypto.

For a theft of this scale, markets stayed remarkably composed.

Bitcoin fell to about $76,400 on Friday, down roughly 4%, and more than $1.3 billion in leveraged positions were liquidated.

That’s a far cry from March 2018, when a failed attack on Binance knocked nearly 11% off Bitcoin in minutes. Analysts credit a bigger, more mature market, now worth around $2.6 trillion, with spot Bitcoin ETFs holding roughly 1.3 million BTC in hands that don’t panic-sell.

Wall Street firms including Cantor Fitzgerald, TD Cowen, and Compass Point even urged clients to buy crypto-linked stocks on the weakness, framing the hack as a Binance security failure rather than a threat to the underlying technology.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig seized on the breach to argue offshore exchanges need U.S. oversight. Selig said in an interview with CNBC that the breach “proves our exact point” behind the crypto-market regulations the agency proposed earlier this month.

And the episode sparked a public spat between Binance founder Changpeng Zhao, better known as CZ, and Elon Musk. The SpaceX and Tesla founder appeared to troll Binance over the hack on X, and CZ fired back, citing Musk’s own SpaceX stake. "Our SPCX shares (from our $500m investment in your Twitter takeover) are worth more than a billion. Would appreciate if you show a little support," CZ posted.

CZ struck a defiant note overall: the theft is the largest in dollar terms, he said, but not in Bitcoin terms, and unlike Mt. Gox or FTX, Binance is absorbing the loss itself.

#5092. Aave borrowers faced up to 36% APR while a freeze blocked fresh collateral

Aave's emergency freeze left borrowers paying interest while blocking the fresh deposits that higher rates normally attract.

Borrowers in Aave's frozen reserves kept paying interest while the restrictions prevented fresh deposits, including the extra collateral they might normally supply to protect a loan. Suppliers were still able to request their money back, provided enough tokens were available, so withdrawals could make borrowing more expensive even though new borrowing was blocked in those reserves.

That left Aave with a rate mechanism whose deposit incentive couldn't work in frozen reserves. More expensive borrowing encourages people to repay and offers better returns to anyone willing to lend, but the freeze prevented that second group from putting money in. Cutting rates could give borrowers some relief, while doing nothing by itself to return the tokens suppliers wanted to withdraw.

The restrictions were introduced during the Oct. 29 Binance hot-wallet incident, and the affected reserves had reopened by Oct. 31.

LlamaRisk's recommendations to reduce borrowing rates temporarily, then restore their earlier settings, explain how protecting a lending market can leave its existing users needing different things from the same pool of money.

Someone using Aave can supply crypto as collateral and borrow another token against it, obtaining funds without selling the original holding. If you want dollars while keeping your Ethereum, for example, you can borrow a dollar-linked token such as USDC.

Those borrowed tokens come from other users who supplied them to Aave's lending contracts, the blockchain programs that handle the transactions. Suppliers earn interest while borrowers owe it. However, the same person can be both a supplier and a borrower with different assets. Supplying ETH as security for a USDC loan doesn't mean you've supplied the USDC that someone else wants to withdraw.

During the Binance hack, a wallet linked to exploiters had already supplied about $60 million of USDT and borrowed 19,762 WETH, according to LlamaRisk's account. The provider described the position as overcollateralized, meaning its collateral exceeded its debt, and said it was subject to normal liquidation. The restrictions were intended to limit further use of Aave during an external compromise.

Freezing the affected reserves stopped new supply and borrowing while repayments, withdrawals, and liquidations were still enabled. Therefore, existing debt continued to accumulate interest, but borrowers couldn't supply extra collateral into those frozen reserves.

Repayment could still reduce the debt, provided the borrower could obtain the necessary tokens. If you've borrowed USDC against ETH, repaying requires USDC, while adding more ETH would ordinarily let you support the debt without reducing it. Losing the second option narrows how you can manage the position, especially if the collateral's market price is falling at the same time.

Under Aave's liquidation rules, the health factor divides collateral value, adjusted by its liquidation threshold, by total debt. Below one, the position becomes eligible for liquidation, allowing another participant to repay debt and receive collateral plus a bonus. Interest adds to the debt side of that calculation even if the collateral price doesn't move.

However, suppliers depended on a different balance: how much of the token they wanted was actually available to withdraw. In a simplified pool with 100 USDC supplied and 90 USDC borrowed, only 10 USDC is immediately unborrowed, even if every loan has ample collateral behind it. Collateral supporting those loans doesn't turn into additional USDC just because a supplier wants to leave.

If suppliers withdraw five of those available tokens while the debt stays at 90 USDC, the pool now has 95 USDC supplied and only five available. The borrowed share has increased from 90% to about 94.7%, despite nobody taking out another loan. This is what utilization measures, and it explains how withdrawals can make a frozen market more expensive for borrowers who haven't touched their accounts.

Aave's rate formula charges more as utilization increases, with a stronger response beyond a configured threshold. Its upper section is controlled by a parameter called Slope2. Normally, higher borrowing costs encourage repayment while better supplier returns can attract fresh funds, and both can replenish the tokens available for withdrawal. During the freeze, new deposits were prohibited, leaving repayment as the available channel among those two responses.

That's why LlamaRisk recommended reducing that upper rate response. Its rationale attributed utilization of 92% to 100% in major stablecoin reserves to supplier withdrawals during the freeze, with annual borrowing rates of 10% to 36% under the settings then in place. Those were annualized rates, so a few hours at 36% didn't add 36% to someone's debt.

To put the scale in dollars, a hypothetical $10,000 loan at a constant 36% annual percentage rate would accrue roughly $9.86 in one day, versus about $2.74 at 10%, ignoring compounding and fees.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Reducing that bill also reduces the interest available to support supplier returns. Suppliers may prefer more interest, but someone trying to withdraw needs the underlying token, and Aave permits withdrawals only against available liquidity, subject to the account's collateral requirements. Even an attractive quoted yield can't pay for something outside the protocol until the supplier can retrieve the tokens.

Once fresh supply was possible again, a higher rate could once more attract deposits, giving LlamaRisk a reason to recommend restoring the earlier upper slopes on Oct. 31. Its published comparison held each reserve's utilization constant to show what the proposed settings would do, rather than recording what borrowers subsequently paid.

Borrowing APR for five Aave V3 USDC reserves at each reserve's Oct. 31, 2026, 18:05 UTC utilization. Compares temporary settings with proposed restored settings, holding utilization fixed within each reserve. These are LlamaRisk's published modeled rates, not a before-and-after time series or realized interest bills. Values retain source rounding; no interpolation.

Source: LlamaRisk, Oct. 31, 2026, Aave V3 comparison at 18:05 UTC

Core USDC was at its 94% threshold, where restoring the upper slope produced no immediate increase in the modeled rate. Base USDC was beyond its own threshold, so the same kind of intervention produced a much higher modeled cost. The reserve containing billions of dollars could show no immediate rate difference while the smaller reserve became more expensive, because each had different utilization and settings.

CryptoSlate's reopening coverage documented the continuing constraints. Permission to deposit or borrow, the collateral accepted for a loan, and the funds available for withdrawal determine different parts of what a user can do.

Getting more tokens back into a reserve also depends on what people actually do with those permissions. Fresh deposits and repayments add available tokens, while new borrowing and supplier withdrawals consume them.

Borrowers trying to preserve collateral consequently have a reason to want slower debt accrual, while suppliers trying to exit have a reason to want stronger repayment incentives.

#5085. How to Run an AI Model Locally—And Why You Might Want To

How to Run an AI Model Locally—And Why You Might Want To

Open-weight AI models now trail the best closed models by only about four months on average, and a growing number of them run on hardware you probably already own.

"Open-weight" means the company publishes the weights, the files holding everything the model learned, so anyone can download and run them. Two free programs, Ollama and LM Studio, handle the rest, and Gemma 4 downloads in Ollama's library range from about 4.6GB to 20GB.

With open models, you don't need to pay $20 a month for an AI subscription or send your conversations to someone else's servers to get a capable chatbot. This guide explains how to download and run AI models directly on your computer, what kind of hardware you'll need, and how to get started with free tools like Ollama and LM Studio.

Whether you want to save money, keep your conversations private, or experiment with AI models you can customize yourself, setting up a local AI assistant may be easier than you think.

LM Studio's documentation says the app "can operate entirely offline, just make sure to get some model files first," and that documents attached to a chat can be used entirely offline too.

Subscriptions to paid chatbots run about $20 per month, while the software here is free. The cost shifts to hardware you may already have.

Licenses decide what you can do with downloaded weights. DeepSeek's R1 series is under the MIT License, and its model card says the models "support commercial use, allow for any modifications and derivative works," while Google's Gemma 4 and OpenAI's gpt-oss ship under Apache 2.0.

Running models on an open-source license like Apache or MIT means you are free to use, modify, and do whatever you want with a model. This means users are able to access improved and modified versions of a specific model that better fit their needs. Maybe you need a model that is good at medicine, so you don’t care about how good it codes. This level of personalization is only available in customized models.

Models are sized in parameters, the internal numbers adjusted during training, and the download grows with them. The model has to fit in your graphics card's video memory, known as VRAM, or in a Mac's unified memory, which the processor and graphics chip share.

As a general rule, you'll want at least 8GB of VRAM, that compressed smaller will be able to fit. However, serious users should buy a gaming GPU with 24GB of VRAM or more, either by stacking up GPUs or by buying specific AI racks or machines with unified memory like the Mac Mini or the Nvidia DGX Spark.

Quantization shrinks a model by storing each weight with fewer bits. Hugging Face's documentation describes it as representing weights and activations "with low-precision data types like 8-bit integer (int8) instead of the usual 32-bit floating point (float32)," which cuts memory use at the cost of some accuracy.

LM Studio's system requirements recommend 16GB or more of RAM on Apple Silicon Macs running macOS 14 or newer, though 8GB Macs can handle smaller models. On Windows it recommends at least 16GB of RAM and 4GB of dedicated VRAM, with AVX2 processor support, and Intel-based Macs are not supported.

Ollama's library lists Gemma 4 26B at 16 to 19GB and the 31B version at 19 to 20GB. OpenAI's gpt-oss-20b needs only 16GB of memory, and gpt-oss-120b runs within 80GB, according to OpenAI.

LM Studio is probably the go-to choice because it provides a nice polished graphical user interface. Ollama, on the other hand, is aimed at developers and more experienced users who prefer working inside of a terminal (CLI).

For LM Studio, download the installer for your operating system from lmstudio.ai, search for a model inside the app, download its weights and open a chat. The app searches Hugging Face, the public hub where open models are published.

For Ollama, macOS and Linux users install with the command curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh, and Windows users run irm https://ollama.com/install.ps1 | iex in PowerShell, both listed on Ollama's download page.

Typing ollama pull gemma4:e2b downloads a model and ollama run gemma4:e2b opens a chat, and /bye exits, per Ollama's quickstart. The program also opens a local API at http://localhost:11434, so other apps on your machine can use the model.

Google's Multi-Token Prediction drafters for Gemma 4, available on Ollama under Apache 2.0, speed up generation by up to 3x using speculative decoding, in which a small model proposes several tokens ahead and the large one checks them together.

Google said that "if the target model agrees with the draft, it accepts the entire sequence in a single forward pass," and the target model adds one extra token of its own in that same pass.

Local models gain web search and file access through MCP servers, short for Model Context Protocol, and LM Studio added support in version 0.3.17, according to the Decrypt guide.

The four-month average describes the most capable open models, and the largest are far past consumer hardware. Alibaba's Qwen3.8-Max has 2.4 trillion total parameters, with 95 billion active at any time, compared with the 80GB of memory that gpt-oss-120b runs within.

When a model spills out of VRAM into system RAM, responses may be a lot slower, and larger context windows need more memory. Gemma 4's small models offer a 128K-token context window, the amount of text a model can consider at once, and the larger ones 256K.

Cloud services need none of that planning, and the frontier models behind them still lead on the hardest tests.

If you want to use AI privately, our own review recommended "xPrivo with a self-hosted local model for day-to-day drafting that must not leave your machine," and Kagi "when you need web retrieval and accept the API call tradeoff."

#5083. New Jersey drops Coinbase staking order ahead of crypto’s midterm test

New Jersey lifted its staking ban and dropped a $5 million penalty, clearing the way for Coinbase to restore staking access for users in the US state.

New Jersey’s securities regulator has rescinded its 2023 cease-and-desist order against Coinbase’s staking service, lifting a ban that stopped residents staking new funds on the exchange for over three years.

The Oct. 30 notice, signed by Acting Bureau Chief Keith A. Alt, withdrew the order and its $5 million penalty without prejudice and without a ruling on the merits. The wording leaves the Bureau of Securities free to bring a fresh enforcement action against Coinbase in the future.

Coinbase disclosed the move in its third-quarter filing, stating the regulator informed the company on Oct. 29 of its plan to rescind. CEO Brian Armstrong said New Jersey users would regain staking access “very soon”.

California, Maryland, Wisconsin and Washington are still pursuing enforcement actions against the exchange with cases against the its staking services.

Staking involves committing cryptocurrency to help validate transactions and secure a blockchain in exchange for rewards. Companies like Coinbase handle the process for customers and take a share of the rewards as a fee.

California led the 10-state task force behind the 2023 actions, which covered more than 600,000 accounts held by Californians.

Like New Jersey, California, Wisconsin and Maryland banned Coinbase from new staking while their cases ran. Washington’s regulator allows staking, but the ongoing case seeks to impose a $4 million fine on the exchange.

Coinbase estimates that California users have missed out on over $89 million in staking rewards since 2023, against more than $15 million in New Jersey.

The decision came ahead of the Nov. 3 US midterm elections, with prediction markets favoring Democrats to win the Senate control. A Democratic majority would put Sen. Elizabeth Warren, a prominent crypto critic, in line to chair the Senate Banking Committee, while Sen. Cynthia Lummis, a leading Republican advocate for the industry, is set to retire in January 2027.

New Jersey’s reversal came under Democratic Gov. Mikie Sherrill, who voted for both the CLARITY and GENIUS bills as a House member. The state’s Democratic senator, Cory Booker, also helped negotiate market structure legislation, though he opposed advancing CLARITY in September.

The industry has sought to build support across party lines ahead of the midterms. Fairshake and its affiliated super PACs are backing 19 Republicans and 14 Democrats in House races, while New Jersey’s Josh Gottheimer has also received crypto-industry support. The number of members of Congress with an A rating from advocacy group Stand With Crypto has risen to 332, up from 96 when the group launched in 2023, according to Reuters.

#5080. Strategy’s preferred-stock fix sets up renewed bitcoin buying, analysts say

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

MSTR fell after third-quarter results, but StoneX and TD Cowen kept buy ratings, citing a $4.83 billion cash reserve and a steadier STRC preferred.

Strategy (MSTR) fell nearly 10% on Friday following its third-quarter earnings, but Wall Street remains bullish on the leading bitcoin treasury company.

Analysts say Strategy has successfully strengthened its balance sheet and stabilized its preferred-stock financing structure, although the effort came at the expense of near-term bitcoin-per-share growth.

Strategy ended the third quarter with $5.07 billion in dollar assets, including a $4.83 billion cash reserve that covers roughly 3.2 to 3.4 years of preferred dividends and debt interest, according to analyst reports from StoneX and TD Cowen.

The company also spent roughly $2 billion repurchasing its STRC preferred stock, helping restore the shares to near their $100 par value and demonstrating greater resilience in its digital credit financing model. However, Strategy's bitcoin per share declined 3.7% year to date as the company prioritized building cash reserves and supporting its preferred securities over accumulating bitcoin.

Separately, shareholders of Strategy's STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD preferred shares approved a change this week allowing dividends to accrue daily, effective Nov. 1 for STRC and Jan. 1 for the other securities.

"Management said the change was intended to reduce volatility, improve liquidity, broaden demand, and move the instruments in the direction of the tokenized, around-the-clock market that they are anticipating," StoneX analyst Mark Palmer wrote.

﻿Expand Chart

With its preferred-stock financing structure more stable and liquidity substantially improved, analysts believe Strategy is better positioned to resume bitcoin accumulation and grow its bitcoin per share.

"We view these developments as important primarily because deeper distribution and better liquidity could ultimately increase Strategy’s capacity to issue Digital Credit efficiently," TD Cowen analysts wrote. "That, rather than STRC's trading price in isolation, remains the connection to MSTR common-equity holders."

StoneX reiterated its buy rating and $435 price target on MSTR, while TD Cowen maintained its buy rating with a $260 price target.

MSTR traded down 8.7% to $129.90 per share on Friday morning. The stock is down approximately 27% year to date.

#5079. Bitcoin ETF Investors Head for the Exits After Record Binance Hack

Bitcoin ETF Investors Head for the Exits After Record Binance Hack

The first real test of how ETF investors would react to the largest crypto hack in history came back red.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $643.2 million on Friday, according to Decrypt’s ETF tracker, their biggest single-day outflow of October. It was the funds’ first full session since attackers began draining roughly $6.4 billion from Binance at 4:05 p.m. ET Thursday, after U.S. markets had closed. Thursday’s $17.2 million in inflows predated the theft.

The selling erased a four-day, $441 million inflow run, leaving the funds down about $202 million for the week and roughly $516 million for October. Cumulative net inflows since launch slipped to $57.4 billion, with total net assets at $103.2 billion.

Ethereum and Solana ETFs fared worse relative to their size. Ethereum funds lost $155.7 million on Friday, extending a five-day outflow streak to about $431 million and pushing October losses to roughly $1.61 billion. Solana ETFs shed $10.8 million, their fifth straight day of outflows, totaling about $72 million for the week. They logged net inflows on just two days all month.

Spot crypto ETFs hold the underlying asset, such as Bitcoin or Ethereum, and trade on stock exchanges like ordinary shares, giving investors exposure through a regular brokerage account.

Bitcoin funds have drawn $57.4 billion in net inflows since debuting in January 2024, and Ethereum funds have taken in $12.2 billion since launching that July. Because issuers report flows daily and much of the money comes from institutions and financial advisers, the figures have become a closely watched gauge of investor sentiment.

Crypto markets initially appeared to take the Binance hack largely in stride, considering the scale of the theft. Bitcoin fell about 4% to roughly $76,400 on Friday, and more than $1.3 billion in leveraged positions were liquidated. It’s a sharp drop, but a far smaller one than the nearly 11% plunge after a failed attack on Binance in 2018.

Binance has pledged to cover all losses with company funds and an emergency reserve, while Tether and Circle froze more than $2.3 billion in stolen stablecoins. Some Wall Street firms even urged clients to buy crypto-linked stocks on the potential weakness in markets.

By Saturday, Bitcoin had steadied at about $77,520, up 0.5% over 24 hours but down 3.3% on the week. Ethereum traded near $2,251, down 3.9% for the week, and Solana near $96, down 4.5%. Total crypto market cap held at about $2.7 trillion.

Macro headwinds remain in play. The Federal Reserve held rates at 3.75% to 4% on Wednesday, but two officials voted for a hike, and traders had priced odds above 70% for an increase in December.

Traders on Myriad, a prediction market developed by Decrypt’s parent company Dastan, have turned bearish on Ethereum and Solana. They now give Ethereum a 51.3% chance of falling to $1,500 before reaching $3,000, flipping a market that favored the upside at 61.3% a week ago. Solana’s odds of sinking to $40 before hitting $160 rose to 53.7%.

#5076. Texas brothers sentenced to 15 years for holding Minnesota family at gunpoint in $8 million crypto heist

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Raymond Christian Garcia and Isiah Angelo Garcia were sentenced to 15 years and 15.5 years, respectively for their roles in that September 2025 armed robbery.

Two brothers who held a family for nine hours at gunpoint in Minnesota for $8 million worth of cryptocurrency were sentenced to about 15 years in prison each, on Friday.

Two Texas men, Raymond Christian Garcia and Isiah Angelo Garcia, were sentenced to 15 years and 15.5 years, respectively, for their roles in that September 2025 armed robbery, according to a statement released by the U.S. Attorney's Office for the District of Minnesota.

This comes after both brothers pleaded guilty to one count of interference with commerce by robbery back in June. In September 2025, Isiah Garcia pressed one of the victims, the father, to log into his crypto accounts and transfer funds to a crypto wallet that his brother then provided. However, the brothers later realized that the father had more crypto funds at a "hard drive-style cryptocurrency wallet" at a cabin three hours away, prosecutors said previously.

Later, the victim's son called 911, and the brothers fled, but were later tracked down by a Wendy's receipt, video surveillance of Raymond Garcia renting a motel room in Roseville, Minnesota, and a rental car, which led police to the brothers' house in Waller, Texas.

FBI Minneapolis Field Office Special Agent in Charge Christopher D. Dotson said the brothers' actions were targeted and "extremely violent."

"Raymond and Isiah Garcia violated the sanctity of a home, abducted a victim, terrorized a family at gunpoint for hours, and disrupted an entire community in a brazen act of violence and greed," Dotson said in a statement on Friday.

#5074. North Korea denies role in $6.3B Binance hack, points finger at US

Pyongyang has pushed back against the FBI’s attribution of the Binance attack, with Kim Yo Jong calling the US allegations politically motivated.

Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, denied North Korea’s involvement in the $6.3 billion Binance hack and appeared to suggest, without evidence, that the US could have been behind the theft.

In a statement released via the Korean Central News Agency (KCNA) on Tuesday, Kim rejected the FBI’s attribution of the attack to a North Korean-backed hacking group.

“The FBI is trying to brand the DPRK as a ‘cyber criminal state’, claiming that it obtained information that a hacking organization ‘supported’ by the DPRK was involved in the incident,” said Kim. “But the US assertion is an absurd rhetoric and nothing new.”

She described the allegations as a political provocation intended to damage North Korea’s international standing. The FBI also blamed North Korea for the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, an accusation Kim also rejected on Tuesday.

“This is not the first time that the U.S. has attempted to spread misunderstanding of the DPRK in the international community, blindly linking the DPRK with the ‘cyber crime’ incidents whenever they occur on an international scale,” said Kim.

Kim also pointed the finger at the US for involvement in the hack.

“If it carefully examines who will get the large amount of the so-called stolen cryptocurrency when the latter is retrieved, it may get a clue about who is behind the hacking incident,” she said.

The statement provided no evidence connecting Washington to the attack. Cointelegraph reached out to the FBI for comment.

Last week, blockchain intelligence firm TRM Labs attributed the Binance attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group with high confidence, citing onchain and offchain intelligence. Other investigators, including Chainalysis and ZachXBT, also linked the theft to North Korean hackers.

TRM Labs pointed to similarities with previous North Korean attacks, including advance preparation, the use of Tornado Cash to fund attacker wallets and the coordinated execution of previously obtained transaction signatures. However, it said the laundering activity differed from the pattern following the Bybit hack.

Onchain investigator ZachXBT also identified a connection between the Binance and Bitget hacks, saying stolen funds from both incidents were commingled at the same address after passing through THORChain. During early investigation into the hack, Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said preliminary findings linked IP addresses to VPN services used “by a certain DPRK group.”

Related: FBI blames North Korea for record $6.3B Binance hack

#5072. FBI Blames North Korea for $6B Binance Hack as Exchange Reopens Withdrawals

FBI Blames North Korea for $6B Binance Hack as Exchange Reopens Withdrawals

The FBI has confirmed that North Korea was behind last week's theft of more than $6 billion from Binance, as the exchange began reopening withdrawals and reported billions of dollars leaving the platform.

In an alert published on Saturday, the FBI said the attack was carried out by North Korean hackers it refers to as "TraderTraitor," confirming the attribution blockchain analytics firms TRM Labs and Chainalysis made on Friday.

The FBI is releasing information advising the Democratic People's Republic of Korea (North Korea) was responsible for the theft of more than $6 billion USD in virtual assets from cryptocurrency exchange, Binance, on or about October 29, 2026. The FBI refers to this specific North… pic.twitter.com/TBUvUFln4n

— FBI Cyber Branch (@FBICyberBranch) October 31, 2026

The FBI, together with the Secret Service and IRS Criminal Investigation, has seen the hackers converting stolen assets mainly into Ethereum, and expects them to launder the funds further through DeFi and instant swap services, the bureau said. It urged node operators, instant exchanges, DeFi services, blockchain analytics firms and other crypto businesses to block transactions involving hundreds of Ethereum addresses, 19 Bitcoin addresses and one Solana address it linked to the theft.

The Secret Service, which is also investigating the theft, said on Sunday that its investigators worked with industry partners to identify and freeze "a significant portion" of the stolen crypto within minutes of the attack. That work continues with the FBI, IRS Criminal Investigation, the U.S. Attorney's Offices for Washington, D.C. and the Central District of California, and the Justice Department's computer crime and national security divisions, the agency said.

The laundering is already well underway. After the attack, the hackers moved more than $500 million on Tron through more than 1,000 addresses, over 3,000 deposits and several Tron-based DeFi protocols, blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis said on Sunday. Most of those staging wallets have now been drained, with funds leaving Tron through cross-chain services including Chainflip, Relay, THORChain and NEAR for dozens of fresh wallets on Ethereum, Bitcoin and other chains, and the launderers have started moving onto Solana, the firm said.

Binance began restoring withdrawals in phases at 1 a.m. ET on Sunday, and all 15 networks and assets in its first phase were available by 7:36 a.m. ET. In the first nine and a half hours, it processed $4.649 billion in withdrawals and $1.01 billion in deposits, a net outflow of about $3.64 billion, the exchange said.

That represents 2.1% of the $171 billion in assets Binance reported in its most recent proof of reserves, from October 28, according to the exchange. "After an initial spike, withdrawal volumes have declined and are stabilising as expected," Binance said. It took a new reserves snapshot on October 31 and expects to publish an updated report on Monday.

As withdrawals began, CEO Richard Teng said Binance had replenished approximately 95% of the affected assets. "Binance will absorb the financial impact of the stolen funds, not our users," he wrote.

Today, we're beginning the phased resumption of withdrawals, taking a careful approach to ensure services return safely and securely.

We've now replenished approximately 95% of the impacted assets, and our commitment remains clear: Binance will absorb the financial impact of the… https://t.co/wKvI7Q4fsT pic.twitter.com/uWFGs3sJJ5

— Richard Teng (@_RichardTeng) November 1, 2026

Binance has since reopened withdrawals on more networks, including Zcash, TON and Sui, and on Monday restored fiat withdrawals in U.S. dollars, UAE dirhams, Vietnamese dong, Philippine pesos and Indonesian rupiah.

"Our top priority right now is to ensure the environment is secure, after which users can withdraw their funds and Binance can resume operations," Binance co-CEO Yi He said on X on Monday, Reuters reported. Binance said it has raised $6 billion to cover the losses from its corporate treasury, an insurance fund and its SAFU emergency fund, according to Reuters, which also reported that Circle had frozen more than $1.1 billion in USDC.

Data from DefiLlama, which tracks the public wallets of exchanges, put Binance's total assets at about $127.6 billion on Monday. More than $6 billion left wallets associated with Binance over 24 hours after withdrawals reopened, CoinDesk reported, citing DefiLlama, though those movements can include transfers between Binance's own wallets. Rival exchanges OKX and Bybit recorded net inflows of about $450 million and $134 million, respectively, during Binance's reopening.

The hack has also rattled institutional investors. Spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $643.2 million on Friday, the first full trading session after the hack, leaving the funds with roughly $516 million in net outflows for October. "A hack at a crypto exchange is bank robbery at the speed of the internet," TRM Labs' Ari Redbord told Reuters.

#5070. President Trump says they’re looking into Binance hack as calls grow for crypto rules

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Friday, The Digital Chamber CEO Cody Carbone sent a letter to House and Senate leadership, demanding that Congress immediately pass a bill that would give the CFTC authority over crypto exchanges.

Following the $6.3 billion hack of the world's largest crypto exchange, Binance, calls are growing for legislation and for firms like Binance to move onshore.

On Thursday, Binance suffered that blow following what its co-CEO Richard Teng said was caused in part by a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop that gave an attacker access to a service container used to submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions. Blockchain analytics firms like TRM Labs have attributed the attack to TraderTraitor, a hacking unit in North Korea.

During a press conference on Friday afternoon, President Donald Trump said he was looking into the hack, adding that Binance has said they are ensuring customers get their funds back.

"But it's a hack," he said. "That's the problem. You know the old-fashioned way when you used to sign a check… these are different people, never see the money anymore. So I've always felt it was a little bit dangerous."

Earlier on CNBC, Commodity Futures Trading Commission Chair Michael Selig pointed to Binance not being federally regulated by his agency as an issue. On Oct. 5, the agency proposed its federal framework for crypto, specifically for leveraged and margined retail crypto trading and a new registration category for exchanges offering those products.

"This proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Selig said. "The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States."

Both Republicans and Democrats have expressed concerns about Binance over the past few years, particularly when it came to money laundering and its consumer protections. In 2023, the exchange's previous CEO Changpeng Zhao pleaded guilty to failing to implement adequate anti-money-laundering controls at Binance as part of a sweeping $4 billion settlement. He was later pardoned by Trump.

Whether or not the Clarity Act, broad crypto legislation to regulate exchanges along with other facets of the industry, would have helped freeze the transactions lost in the Binance hack also came up. The bill was passed by the House last year, but has since stalled in the Senate amid concerns over Trump's vast crypto wealth. That wealth had grown to hundreds of millions of dollars linked to World Liberty Financial and his TRUMP memecoin and became something many Democrats could not ignore in trying to pass the bill.

"Yesterday, hackers stole more than $6 billion from Binance in less than 4 hours, the biggest heist in history," said one of the bill's negotiators, Sen. Cynthia Lummis, R-Wyo., in a post on X. "The Clarity Act would have given crypto firms legal cover to freeze suspicious transactions the instant law enforcement calls. Every minute lost is money gone for good."

Amanda Fischer, policy director and chief operating officer for Better Markets and who was the previous chief of staff at the Securities and Exchange Commission, pushed back, saying that the bill would have required stablecoin issuers to "freeze per a court order," adding that at the same time crypto firms worked as quickly as they could to freeze the assets, in a post on X.

However, Solana Policy Institute General Counsel Patrick Wilson said that wasn't right and that a section in the Clarity Act "expressly protected stablecoin issuers from private lawsuits for voluntary, temporary holds based on a reasonable belief of a violation of law. No court order required."

On Friday, The Digital Chamber CEO Cody Carbone sent a letter to House and Senate leadership, demanding that Congress immediately pass a bill that would give the CFTC authority over crypto exchanges and keep customer funds and company funds separate from each other in bankruptcy proceedings.

"No federal regulator can set and enforce consistent standards for how these platforms hold, segregate, and secure customer assets, or for their cybersecurity, before consumers are harmed," Carbone said. "Regulators can only act after the damage is done."

"We urge you to act before this Congress adjourns," he added.

Rep. Tom Timmons, R-S.C., meanwhile, brought up national security concerns.

"America cannot allow rogue regimes to exploit digital assets to finance their nuclear weapons and ballistic missile programs," Timmons said in a post on X.

#5067. Inside the largest crypto hack on record: How attackers breached Binance for $6.3 billion

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Onchain investigators suggest the attackers exploited Binance's internal systems and wallet-signing mechanisms to move funds across multiple blockchains.

Binance was hit Thursday by the largest crypto hack to date, with blockchain investigators estimating that more than $6 billion in assets were stolen. Beyond the financial toll, the breach has raised questions about the security of centralized exchange infrastructure, particularly the hot wallets used to custody billions of dollars in customer assets.

Binance said its initial investigation pointed to a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop and unauthorized access to a service container. The exchange has not identified a private-key leak, while co-CEO Richard Teng has described the attack as more complex than a compromise of a single employee's permissions.

Blockchain investigators have attributed the attack to North Korean hackers. Their analysis suggests the attackers exploited Binance's internal systems and wallet-signing mechanisms to move funds across multiple blockchains.

The attacker began staging at least 36 hours before the theft, funding an operational wallet with small Tornado Cash withdrawals, according to TRM Labs. It deployed and tested malicious code on three wallets before executing the attack.

On Ethereum, the attacker used EIP-7702 to turn a Binance hot wallet into a sweeper controlled by the attacker. It prepared 50 pre-signed authorizations to ensure one would remain valid when executed. TRM said coordinated transfers across nine other blockchains suggest a compromise of Binance's broader hot-wallet signing infrastructure.

Elliptic's analysis suggests the attacker retained access beyond the initial exploit, using one compromised wallet's owner rights to withdraw funds from another Binance wallet and repeatedly sweep new deposits. A second Ethereum hot wallet also sent $174 million in assets directly to the attacker, further pointing to access to Binance's broader transaction-signing infrastructure.

Simone Imbrescia, vice president of Immunefi, said the exploit highlights how an attack's sophistication does not necessarily lie in its onchain code, but in gaining access to the infrastructure capable of authorizing transactions.

"One of the striking things about this breach is how little code was actually needed on-chain. The sweeper contract was only around 130 lines," Imbrescia said. "The hard part sometimes is getting access to a system powerful enough to make very simple code devastating."

Chainalysis, Elliptic, and TRM attributed the attack to North Korean hacking groups, with TRM explicitly linking it to the same group connected to the $1.5 billion Bybit hack.

Chainalysis identified onchain links between funds stolen from Binance and proceeds from last month's Bitget exploit, which was also attributed to North Korean hackers. TRM cited similarities to previous North Korean operations, including Tornado Cash funding, advance staging and pre-signed authorizations. Elliptic also cautioned that the onchain overlap with the Bitget incident involved only a small portion of the Binance funds and did not conclusively establish that the same attackers were responsible.

Chainalysis identified thousands of transactions in a short period, with stolen funds repeatedly circulating between addresses. The attacker also attempted to avoid stablecoin freezes by converting $325 million in stablecoins into $285 million in ETH over nearly 20 minutes.

Elliptic said the attacker deposited $59.9 million in stolen USDT into Aave as collateral and borrowed 19,762 ETH. The loan remained open and was close to liquidation as of the firm's latest update. The attacker also swapped hundreds of millions of dollars in USDT for ETH through decentralized exchanges and market makers, accepting losses of as much as 26% against market prices to move funds into assets that issuers cannot freeze.

Aave said Friday that it had unfrozen all markets across its V3 and V4 protocols, adding that its smart contracts were unaffected and user positions remained fully collateralized.

Binance has approximately $5 billion in affected assets, with another $1.3 billion being acquired through market and OTC channels as part of its commitment to cover the losses.

Teng said the exchange was targeting Monday to publish its initial proof-of-reserves report and was working to restore withdrawals.

"We are very hopeful that users will be able to withdraw prior to our Sunday deadline, which is on track to be within 40 hours," Teng said in an X video on Friday.

TD Cowen analysts said the breach highlights the custody and cybersecurity risks associated with centralized exchanges but does not necessarily undermine the broader digital asset industry.

"The evidence available so far points to a compromise of infrastructure at a centralized intermediary, rather than a failure of Bitcoin or its underlying network," the analysts wrote. "Put differently, we do not stop using dollars when a bank is robbed, just as car theft does not invalidate the automobile."

The incident also highlights the need to secure the systems and processes that control access to private keys, Imbrescia said. Crypto security efforts often focus on protecting keys and auditing smart contracts, but the people, infrastructure and workflows capable of instructing those keys are part of the security perimeter, she added.

"If an attacker compromises that operational layer, they may not need to steal a key or discover an exotic smart-contract vulnerability at all," she said. "The broader lesson is that access controls, internal infrastructure and transaction-authorisation processes need to be secured with the same intensity as the keys themselves."
