#5027. DTCC’s tokenization launch could accelerate adoption and boost Securitize, StoneX says

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

DTCC's permissioned service targets different markets than Securitize's public-blockchain tokenization, StoneX analysts said, keeping a buy rating on SECZ.

The Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation's (DTCC) new tokenization service validates rather than threatens Securitize's business model, according to StoneX analysts, who argue that the two platforms serve different markets.

While DTCC's permissioned service is initially limited to certain securities, participants and blockchain networks, Securitize (SECZ) focuses on public-blockchain tokenization, including tokenized funds, equities and assets that can be used in decentralized finance.

"We acknowledge that DTCC has opened a debate about where tokenized U.S. equities should ultimately be anchored," analyst Mark Palmer wrote Monday in a note to clients. "At the same time, we believe SECZ is well positioned to emerge as a winner of that debate because it is the only tokenization platform that already holds the full regulatory stack that an issuer needs to sponsor its own on-chain shares, an SEC-registered transfer agent, broker-dealer and ATS."

StoneX also pointed to the potential for tokenized assets to serve as collateral across markets, arguing that the ability to move tokenized Treasuries around the clock could drive demand for tokenized fund shares and equities as collateral. Securitize is well positioned to benefit, the analysts said, given its regulatory infrastructure and role as a transfer agent for a large pool of tokenized assets.

That use case is already developing in decentralized finance. Tokenized private credit on Solana reached $1.44 billion, with credit tokens from Securitize and Maple being accepted as collateral in lending markets such as Kamino, according to The Block's latest Tokenization Briefing.

Securitize is also expanding its tokenized equity business. On Monday, the company expanded its tokenized U.S. stock offering to 25 equities, adding 13 companies, including ExxonMobil, GameStop, Salesforce and Disney, to its existing lineup. The tokens trade on Solana and are backed 1:1 by underlying shares, providing eligible investors with associated economic benefits and rights, including dividends and voting rights where applicable.

Last month, StoneX argued that Securitize Stocks could help the company move beyond integration fees and generate transaction and servicing revenue, with collateral-related revenue a potential longer-term opportunity.

The firm reiterated its "buy" rating and $18 price target on SECZ shares. The stock traded around $7.30 at publication time, down 24% since going public in July 2025.

The exchange paused deposits and withdrawals after the incident, while Tether froze more than 4.5 million USDT linked to the attacker.

On Saturday, hackers hijacked a dozen DeFi websites registered with Name.com and stole about $138,000. Blockaid said the sites were rigged with a malware kit that tricks visitors into signing away their funds.

#5023. Elon Musk Mocks Sam Altman Over Claim Home PCs Are Wasting RAM

Elon Musk Mocks Sam Altman Over Claim Home PCs Are Wasting RAM

Elon Musk has mocked OpenAI CEO Sam Altman for arguing that the world's limited supply of computer memory would be better used in AI data centers than in people's home computers.

Altman made the comments in an interview with the YouTube channel Cheeky Pint, while discussing the shortages of memory and other components caused by the AI boom.

"A lot of people want local inference, and somehow they assume that the problem is solved now because we're going to have open-source models," Altman said, referring to running AI models on a user's own hardware. "But from like a global allocation perspective, actually, it's quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world's limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time."

"You want maximum utilization in a cloud if you're trying to deal with this very supply-constrained environment," he added. "And somehow, many people's intuition is the opposite of that."

Musk responded on X on Sunday with a meme showing Hans Landa, the Nazi officer played by Christoph Waltz in the film Inglourious Basterds, captioned: "You are withholding DRAM from the cloud, are you not?" "The people deserve to have their RAM too!" Musk wrote alongside the image.

The people deserve to have their RAM too! pic.twitter.com/q6dwauALw4

In the interview, Altman blamed the shortage partly on memory makers' caution. For years, a DRAM producer "would invest $20 billion of CapEx to make $19 billion," he said, and companies that nearly went bankrupt building too many factories in past cycles have been slow to expand, even as they now stand to generate huge profits.

Altman said he would like to see prices for energy, chips and the rest of the supply chain fall. "If it doesn't go down, I'll be very sad," he said, adding that for prices to fall, supply needs to grow.

The jab is the latest in a long-running feud between the two men, who co-founded OpenAI with others in 2015 before Musk left in 2018.

Musk sued OpenAI and Altman in 2024, accusing them of abandoning the organization's founding nonprofit mission. In May, a federal jury in California rejected the $150 billion lawsuit, finding OpenAI, Altman and president Greg Brockman not liable on all claims. OpenAI's lawyers had argued that the suit was motivated in part by competition, after Musk launched rival AI company xAI in 2023.

#5021. Crypto.com to withdraw Hong Kong consumer app citing regulatory changes

Crypto.com will discontinue its consumer app services in Hong Kong in February next year.

Update (Nov. 3, 2026, 11:10 am UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from a Crypto.com spokesperson.

Crypto.com will discontinue its consumer app services in Hong Kong on Feb. 1, 2027, while continuing to pursue regulatory approval for its institutional exchange.

On Tuesday, a Crypto.com spokesperson told Cointelegraph that the company would discontinue its consumer app services in Hong Kong. The spokesperson said all affected customers had received advance notice and would receive instructions on closing their accounts and support to withdraw their funds.

According to local news outlet HK01, Crypto.com cited regulatory changes in emails sent to its users in Hong Kong. The consumer app is operated by Foris DAX MT Limited, while a separate entity, Foris DAX HK Limited, is seeking a Hong Kong virtual asset trading platform license.

According to the Securities and Futures Commission’s (SFC) public register, the company’s Hong Kong entity submitted its VATP license application on Feb. 9, 2024. Nearly three years later, Foris DAX HK Limited remains listed as “deemed-to-be-licensed” — a transitional status rather than full regulatory approval.

The spokesperson said the shutdown reflects Crypto.com’s efforts to optimize global operations and allocate resources toward sustainable long-term opportunities. However, the statement did not specify which regulatory changes prompted the decision.

Crypto.com also plans to continue pursuing the VATP license application for its institutional platform. The company said it remains committed to the broader Asia-Pacific region and working with regulators worldwide to support responsible industry growth.

#5020. Kim Jong-Un’s sister rejects FBI claims linking North Korea to record $6 billion Binance hack: reports

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Monday, Yonhap News Agency reported that hackers from North Korea were not behind the over $6 billion hack of Binance, citing the sister of North Korea's leader Kim Jong-Un.

A North Korean official has denied the country was behind the largest crypto hack on record, despite onchain investigators and the FBI attributing the $6 billion exploit to North Korean hackers.

On Monday, Yonhap News Agency reported that Kim Yo-jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong-Un and a senior government official, rejected the FBI's assessment that North Korea was responsible for last week's Binance hack.

"The FBI is trying to brand the DPRK as a 'cyber criminal state', claiming that it obtained information that a hacking organization 'supported' by the DPRK was involved in the incident," Kim said in a statement, according to Yonhap.

The FBI did not immediately respond to a request for comment from The Block.

Ahead of the attack last week, an attacker deployed and tested malicious code on three wallets before later executing the attack on Binance on Thursday. On Ethereum, the attacker used EIP-7702 to turn a Binance hot wallet into a sweeper controlled by the attacker. It prepared 50 pre-signed authorizations to ensure one would remain valid when executed, according to TRM Labs.

Three firms, including TRM Labs, attributed the attack to North Korean bad actors. Then, over the weekend, the FBI confirmed that North Korea was responsible for the hack. In its release, the FBI referred to this specific North Korean malicious cyber activity as "TraderTraitor."

Earlier on Monday, Binance published its first set of proof of reserves and said it replenished about 95% of the assets affected by the hack with its own funds.

#5018. In Brooklyn Decker and Andy Roddick’s North Carolina Home, Southern Hospitality Meets Modern Charm

The home was, decidedly, not love at first sight for Brooklyn Decker and Andy Roddick. “One of the rooms had caved in; there was water pouring in through the ceiling,” Decker says of the first time the couple saw the Charlotte, North Carolina, Georgian-style house.

But even in its dilapidated state, the 1960s-era home was still intriguing: It was located in the favored neighborhood for the young family, and its traditional, elegant proportions were still evident through its disrepair. At first it was Roddick, the former number-one men’s singles tennis player, who urged Decker to keep the house on their short list. Soon model and actor Decker, who grew up in nearby Matthews, warmed at the chance to rehabilitate a proper Carolina home with details like dental crown molding and classic brick. “We’d moved so many times [before this house],” she says. “And we were excited to try something new. For us, that meant a historic house with some old Southern bones.”

In the entry, Roddick is seated on a vintage sofa covered in a Zak and Fox fabric. Decker stands next to the walnut staircase built by Masterpiece Staircase and finished by Tony Montognese. The custom chandelier is by Kalin Asenov, the rug is vintage, and the artwork is by Kieran Brennan Hinton.

The family’s living room, clad in mahogany paneling, is Decker and Roddick’s favorite space in the whole home. The fireplace was designed by Decker, fabricated by Tyler Ernsberger, and stained by Tony Montognese, with animal carvings done by Stan Dunham. The 1930s coffee table is by Paul László, and the light fixture is a vintage Longobard for Poliarte. The chairs, clockwise from top right, are by BDDW, vintage Swedish sourced from PRB Collection, and a vintage Guglielmo Ulrich.

The home’s foyer received the biggest glow-up, with the staircase being entirely redesigned from a curving one. Chandelier and flush-mount light designed by Decker and Kalin Asenov, sideboard by BDDW, the mirror and sofa are vintage, millwork by Bill Truitt Woodworks (with finishes by Tony Montognese), and the walnut flooring is by Richard Marshall Fine Flooring.

Decker and Roddick were no strangers to home renovation, but as evidenced just from their first walkthrough, this latest undertaking would be on a scale they had not yet explored. The home had been little touched since its construction in 1964, and the couple would be only the second owners. Aside from obvious fixes like leaking ceilings and sagging floors, the layout, which was chopped up into a warren of tight quarters, needed a full gut to allow the space to breathe and gain more light. “We were parents of two young kids,” Decker says. “So the question was, How do we respect the grandeur of the house but also make it feel hospitable to a young family and one that can live up to the demands of everyday life?”

The dining room shows off many of the home’s thematic colors, including red, yellow, and green. Drapery fabric by Kravet, the pink chairs are vintage Kerstin Hörlin Holmquist, dining chairs by Studio Valle de Valle, antique farm table sourced from Obsolete, painting by Tyler Buck Guinn, and the pendants are by Of Enso, made with a vintage silk gingham from Decker’s personal collection.

The first-floor front room continues the nature theme thanks to its hand-painted custom mural. The sofa is a vintage Carl Malmsten upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric; the blue club chairs are vintage and sourced from Eneby Home; the leather chair is a vintage Hans Wegner; coffee table designed by Decker and fabricated by Dunham and Ernsberger Custom Cabinetry; and the armoire is a vintage walnut wet bar sourced from 1stDibs.

The kitchen hall’s allover paneling was fabricated by Bill Woodworks and installed by Blockhaus. Flush mounts by The Urban Electric Co.; the painting is a vintage William Skilling; sideboard by Bicyclette; and the rug is vintage found on Etsy.

The kitchen is painted in a muted shade of Decker’s favorite color: green. The double islands were designed by Decker and Dunham and fabricated by Ernsberger Custom Cabinetry; the pendants are by Roman and Williams Guild; the fittings by Waterworks; and the painting is an antique signed by Adelaide Briggs.

The breakfast nook brings the garden inside with its collection of plants and green accents. The table was custom-designed by Decker and Dunham and fabricated by Ernsberger Custom Cabinetry. The chairs are by Studio Ashby and upholstered with a Lee Jofa fabric, and the light fixture is a vintage Hans-Agne Jackobsson.

To help with the transformation, the couple engaged both Ruard Veltman Architecture for the architectural design and Stanwick Dunham for contracting. The group transformed the nearly 7,000-square-foot structure into a home that celebrates its lineage without being mired in the past. Nowhere is this effort more evident than in the gracious entryway, whose original carpeted staircase curved up from stark black and white tiles. And while that design wasn’t in poor taste, its severity read as both formal and cold. The solution was twofold: both to square-off the staircase and to clad the entire area in rich walnut paneling. The result not only allows sunlight to flow down from the second story, but showcases warm millwork. “We wanted it to look like it predated our arrival, and also be a bit more humble in its materials,” Decker says.

The primary bedroom’s wood floors and cream walls are softened up with the addition of a full wall of drapery, with fabric from William Morris. The chandelier is a vintage Carlo Scarpa sourced from 1stDibs, the bed from The Expert, linens from Nordic Knots, chair by Commune for George Smith, and the painting is by Alexandra Valenti.

Another view of the bedroom spotlights the family’s dog finding his light. Art by Tyler Hays Studio, nightstands by BDDW, vintage daybed from 1stDibs, and the vintage chair is sourced from Evan Reid.

The couple’s closet displays shoes almost in a museum-like setting, inside an antique French shoe cabinet sourced from 1stDibs. Ottoman from John Derian, the paint color is Farrow & Ball White Tie, and the chandelier is from RH.

The home’s primary bath was completely gut-renovated, but Decker wanted the design to appear original to the house. Tile from Waterworks, tub from Water Monopoly, side table by BDDW, painting by George Buckner of The Florida Highwaymen, and the pendant is an antique French copper lantern sourced from 1stDibs.

For the home’s interior design, Decker had no need of hiring a professional: She’s become a decorator herself. Over the last six or so years and mostly by word of mouth, the actor has spearheaded a variety of design projects (her latest, along with her design partners at By Idiots, being the interiors of Intown Golf Club’s new properties in Houston and Raleigh). For her family home, the brief was to keep the Georgian influence while updating the aesthetic. And it remains an evolving one: “Whenever I see that furniture dolly out, I know I’ll be involved at some point,” Roddick says.

Decker achieved the cozy yet elevated mix through color, using muted shades of green (her color), red, blue, and yellow; wallpaper with natural images like leaves or rivers; and plenty of vintage patterned rugs to soften up the wood floors. “It was originally old English meets early Georgian meets Shaker,” she says. “But when our kids became older, we really wanted to incorporate and take seriously what they wanted. Because, how cool to be eight and actually have some say in the space around you? So now the house is a hodgepodge of explosive color, a lot of which is totally inspired and dictated by them.”

The children’s playroom has a vintage feel while still being cozy. Sofa by RH, mohair chair and ottoman by Sam Klemick, wallpaper by Charles Voysey, and the shades are Fabricut fabric.

The couple’s daughter wanted her new room to match her softball team’s colors of red, white, and blue. Bed designed by Decker and fabricated by Liven UP, vintage chandelier sourced from PRB Collection, drapes from Anthropologie, and the rug is sourced from Hall Pass Vintage.

Decker’s office adds a bit of whimsy with a ceiling papered in a Pierre Frey design. Rug by Studio MRS for Nordic Knots, sofa by Coley Home, the desk is vintage, and the chandelier is by Apparatus.

An upstairs landing is one of Decker’s favorite places to snuggle with the family pets. Pendant by Roman and Williams Guild, dresser by Orior, the chairs are vintage Paolo Buffa upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric, table is vintage sourced from PRB Collection, and the pillows were made by Chancery Custom with vintage tapestry fabric sourced by Decker.

#5017. Nvidia and Nike Lead Biggest Wave Yet of Tokenized Stock Objections

Nvidia and Nike Lead Biggest Wave Yet of Tokenized Stock Objections

Even Nvidia doesn’t want its shares on OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

The chipmaker is among 12 new companies listed as objecting issuers in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Friday. The others are Costco, Nike, Micron, CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks, Reddit, Hewlett Packard Enterprise, Corning, Bloom Energy, IREN, and USA Rare Earth. The additions bring the total to 50 companies.

It’s the largest single batch yet. The list stood at 38 in the venue’s Oct. 28 notice, when Walmart and Eli Lilly joined, and at nine when the first objectors appeared on Oct. 15. It has more than quintupled since.

The newcomers span chips, cybersecurity, retail, and energy. Nvidia, Micron, and Corning join hardware peers such as AMD, Intel, and Broadcom, while CrowdStrike and Palo Alto Networks bring cybersecurity heavyweights to the list. IREN, a Bitcoin miner that has expanded into AI cloud computing, adds a name from the crypto industry itself.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to let approved users trade tokenized U.S. stocks around the clock. Trades would run through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

None of the objecting companies have explained their decisions, but Nasdaq offered one possible rationale when it opted its own shares out of the exemption.

A spokesperson told The Wall Street Journal that Nasdaq doesn’t want its stock trading on a venue that doesn’t show the best prices in real time or report trades to the consolidated tape.

Further, Crypto in America reported that Nasdaq may be playing a role behind the scenes in encouraging companies to opt out of the OKX-ICE venture. “Our Nasdaq rep had reached out to us regarding OKX listing, suggesting we should object and that they would hold a call to discuss why,” an executive at a Nasdaq-listed company told the publication. “Nasdaq is definitely partly behind this,” the exec reportedly said.

OKXICE’s notice acknowledges it isn’t subject to Regulation NMS, the rules designed to ensure investors get the best displayed prices. It also warns that broker-dealers face unsettled questions about trade reporting and best execution on the venue.

Some of the market’s biggest names, including Apple, Tesla and Amazon, still haven’t appeared on the list.

#5015. Ark Invest buys $3.4 million in Coinbase, adds Circle and Robinhood as stocks trade lower

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Cathie Wood's Ark Invest bought $3.4 million worth of Coinbase shares, $2.6 million worth of Circle, and $645,820 worth of Robinhood on Monday.

Cathie Wood-led investment firm Ark Invest increased its holdings in Coinbase, Circle, and Robinhood through its exchange-traded funds on Monday, as all three stocks closed lower.

According to Ark's latest trading disclosure, the company purchased 24,790 Coinbase Coinbase Global, Inc. (COIN) shares, worth $3.4 million, through its ARKK, ARKW, and ARKF ETFs. Coinbase shares closed down 3.98% at $137.83 on Monday.

Ark bought 35,657 shares of Circle Circle Internet Group, Inc. (CRCL), worth $2.6 million, as the stock dropped 0.6%. It also added 7,703 Robinhood Robinhood Markets, Inc. (HOOD) shares, worth $645,820 based on Monday's closing price.

Meanwhile, the firm sold 104,172 shares of Bullish Bullish (BLSH), worth $2.3 million. The crypto exchange's shares fell 5.69% to close at $22.54.

Ark's moves came days after Coinbase reported strong third-quarter earnings last week. The company's Q3 net income rose 66% year on year to $719 million from $433 million in the same period last year. It generated $1.22 billion in total revenue, broadly flat from the previous quarter but down 35% year over year.

Notably, Coinbase's prediction markets business surpassed $200 million in annualized revenue, doubling from roughly $100 million in the previous quarter. Bernstein analysts said Friday that the growth in the company's prediction markets and subscription and services revenue "helped offset the softer crypto trading activity."

Circle is scheduled to report its Q3 earnings on Nov. 4. Consensus estimates call for revenue of $788.17 million and EPS of $0.37, according to TradingView. Robinhood reported a 20% jump in crypto revenue from the previous quarter to $120 million, offsetting a decline in crypto trading revenue, according to its results released last week. Meanwhile, Bullish is expected to report Q3 earnings on Nov. 18.

#5013. 14 Best Airbnbs in the South of France for Bucolic Romance (2026)

The South of France has long been shorthand for bucolic romance. The region has drawn painters, film stars, and sun-starved travelers for generations, seducing them with endless vineyards and lavender fields, sleepy medieval villages, and its glittering Riviera. From Provence to the Côte d’Azur, the south of France offers a storybook mélange of rustic farmhouses, centuries-old castles and estates, along with honey-hued stone houses tucked into timeless hilltop towns.

Whether you fancy swimming in a pool besides your own château, late evening pétanque games at a centuries-old castle, gathering a group of friends at a hillside estate, or cozying up in a rustically romantic village apartment, Airbnb has options for turning every south of France fantasy into a reality. Here are some of the most extraordinary homes for rent in the region—spots so tempting, you might just want to cancel your return ticket and settle in for a year in Provence.

Perks: Mountain views, lake access, sauna, balcony, hot tub, indoor and outdoor fireplaces, pool table

While the South of France may be synonymous with summer, this contemporary chalet makes a compelling case for a winter escape with floor-to-ceiling windows that frame Sancy Valley’s mountain views. There’s plenty of room for up to 10 guests to spread out in this five bedroom home with a generous living space that includes three bathrooms and a pool table. Tucked in the hilly countryside, proximity to both Alpine and Nordic skiing is the property’s main draw for those looking to hit the slopes. After a day spent outside, both the indoor fireplace or sauna provide a perfect spot to warm up, though you could also enjoy the fresh air on the expansive balcony while keeping toasty by the outdoor fireplace or hot tub. From $1,048 per night.

Perks: Private backyard, fireplace, sound system, fully-equipped kitchen, security system, free parking, cleaning available for a fee

Located at the foot of Signal du Mailhebiau in the Aubrac region, this two-bedroom getaway is immersed in its rural landscape, surrounded by rolling fields, grazing cattle, and the French Countryside’s natural beauty. Originally built as a buron, or shepherd’s hut, the home has been thoughtfully transformed into a modern retreat that celebrates its architectural history. Hand-laid stone walls, vaulted ceilings, and a loft preserve the structure’s rustic character, while a dramatic wall of windows introduces a contemporary counterpoint, framing picturesque views of the expansive countryside. From $484 per night.

Perks: Private terrace, outdoor dining area, free parking, breakfast baskets delivered by request

Once the orangery of a stately chateau, this converted studio apartment retains its romantic design with soaring ceilings, a wall of French doors, and cheerful green-and-white checkerboard floor. Today, the space is an elegant retreat for two (or a couple with a baby thanks to the available crib), whether you’re spending leisurely afternoons indoors, sipping wine on the terrace, or exploring the surrounding vineyards and forests. For those looking to wander or partake in the region’s rich wine history, you’re about a 30-minute drive to the center of Bordeaux. From $165 per night.

Perks: Vineyard views, pool, outdoor shower, private backyard, fully-equipped kitchen, free parking, cleaning available for a fee

This isn’t so much a home to stay in as a castle to occupy. Situated on nearly ten acres of private parkland, this spacious 16th century château sleeps eight, while an additional two cottages can accommodate another eight guests. A large swimming pool, sun-drenched terrace, and lawn dotted with cypress trees practically beg you to throw a summer soirée. Inside, Château du Hameau’s historic bones remain prominent with original stone flooring, sweeping stairway, and ornate coiffured ceilings. With multiple fireplaces, soaking tubs, and seemingly endless views of fields and vineyards, this is a place to dream about long after you leave. From $1,825 per night.

Perks: Private tennis court, private pool, concierge service

Rustic architecture meets contemporary comfort at the sprawling Mas de l’Aiguebrun Estate, a super-sized stay with enough space to house up to 15 lucky guests. Old world design features like exposed brick and wood beam ceilings that date back to the 11th century contrast modern amenities like a La Cornue oven, waterfall showers, and even a mini rock climbing wall to entertain younger guests. The property has a prime location in Bonnieux, a village with an abundance of restaurants and cafes, as well as a Friday morning market that visitors swear by for local produce and baked goods. Back at the Airbnb, you’re encouraged to spend sunny days en plein air taking advantage of the picturesque outdoor amenities, which include an impressive stone-carved outdoor dining setup, 20-meter swimming pool, and a red clay tennis court to channel your inner Nadal. From $2,150 per night.

Perks: Private terrace, updated air conditioning, complimentary tea and coffee

Natural light floods this 16th century townhouse located in Saint-Paul-de-Vence, a village whose most famous residents include the likes of Pablo Picasso and Henri Matisse. Beautiful interiors are expected when your host is an interior designer, and this property delivers spatial excellence with considered details like a restrained color palette, layered textures, and contemporary furnishings. The combined kitchen and dining area is a perfect place to enjoy slow mornings (in fact, we think the envy-inducing pottery shelf is an idea you’ll want to recreate in your own space). A copper bath tub faces a king-sized bed in one of the main bedrooms, complete with a window view overlooking the surrounding foliage. For more private guests, the property also has three en suite bathrooms in a glassy green color palette, each housing their own tubs and showers. If you’re hoping to explore your charming provincial surroundings, lots of art and shopping attractions await; plus, the 40-minute drive to Nice makes it convenient to plan a day trip or two. From $649 per night.

Gilded light fixtures, muted textiles, and farmhouse furniture all distinguish this abundantly pastoral stay on the French Riviera. While there’s plenty of classic provincial charm to immerse yourself in—exposed wood beams, stone flooring, and two fireplaces—the design also ties in moments of splashy Mediterranean character with coastal furnishings and colorful tilework. It’s worth stopping by the local markets to gather ingredients for a meal or two in the fully-equipped kitchen, with its pine green cabinetry, Smeg stove and matching dishwasher, as well as a dishware collection rounded out by plenty of Serax ceramics for a festive dinner party. When you are looking to venture out, the surrounding Roquefort les Pins offers a small community of indie stores run by locals, and eateries, while more lively attractions in Nice, Antibes, and Cannes are just a 30 minute drive away. From $693 per night.

Perks: Sleeps a crowd, heated pool, pétanque court, chef, cleaners, and airport transfer available upon request, close to wineries and a golf course

This nine-bedroom complex sleeps a crowd, but no matter how many people you arrive with, you’re bound to be struck by the ultra-private feeling of the estate. The centuries-old stone buildings surrounded by cypress trees originally comprised a farm, but today have been converted into three buildings fit for at least 16 guests. Inside, the oversized rooms are filled with cushy leather couches, bentwood chairs, and cozy beds. Outdoors, the scenic grounds are just right for a heated game of pétanque or a dip in the heated pool. From $2,894 per night.

Perks: Sleeps a crowd, heated pool, air conditioning, indoor fireplace, crib, cleaning available during your stay, pizza oven

If you’ve been daydreaming of your very own French estate, it’s entirely possible you’ve manifested this Airbnb into being. Nestled in the Provençal countryside near the village of Gordes, the 12-bedroom home offers striking mountain views alongside a range of picturesque outdoor perks that include a heated pool, pétanque court, and an elegant garden. The home’s original features have been thoughtfully maintained, from the exposed stone walls to the vaulted ceilings, while the interiors lean into pastoral elegance with farmhouse furnishings and creamy linens. Not too far from the villa, you can traipse through the infamous lavender fields and vineyards that have become synonymous with the region. From $959 per night.

Perks: Ocean-front views, central AC, swimming pool, security system

This palatial estate on the French Riviera might be large enough for a royal wedding, but that doesn’t mean you can’t book the six-bedroom home all for yourself. Everything about this rental is over-the-top, from the palm trees that flank the front door to the grand entry hall complete with marble columns, a sweeping staircase, and ceiling frescos. Staying here feels a bit like having a castle all to yourself, one where you can while away the days outside in the gardens, by the pool, or along the nearby shores. The grand, high-ceilinged spaces interiors are outfitted with antique furnishings and chandeliers, along with an elective blend of mid-century pieces and lots of artwork. Should the mood strike for a soirée fit for a king, the home’s living room and conservatory can easily accommodate 200 for a cocktail party. From $10,350 per night.

Perks: Sleeps a crowd, extensive grounds, heated pool, cleaning available during your stay

For those “go big or go home” vacations, go ahead and book a grand castle. This one is from the 17th century and encompasses over 300 acres of forest, farmland, and vineyards. The property itself features nine bedrooms, most which have ensuite bathrooms with soaking tubs, terracotta and stone floors, exposed stone walls, and bright and airy rooms doused in bright colors like marigold to cerulean. Inside and out, there are multiple seating areas, including a music room and dedicated office, to gather with guests (accommodating over a dozen people at any given time). From $2,458 per night.

Perks: Sleeps a crowd, piano, washer and dryer, pool, EV charger, exercise equipment, pool and pink pong tables, outdoor dining area

This sprawling farmhouse just outside the village of Mouriès sleeps at least 16 people and has plenty of room, both inside and out, for everyone to sprawl out. The 10 bedrooms all come with en-suite bathrooms, and there are multiple living rooms for gathering in smaller groups—ones accented in antique tiles, marble fireplaces, vaulted stone ceilings, and sumptuous couches. The most striking room in the home is the dining room, with its geometrically wall mural, exposed antique wooden ceiling beams, and a table that seats 26. From $3,447 per night.

Perks: Long term stays available, washer and dryer, located in a walkable village

The whole space, from its colorful limewashed walls to its checkerboard kitchen, give off the feel of an artist’s retreat. And if a true retreat is what you’re looking for, feel free to linger a while (long-term stays are very welcome here). From $359 per night.

#5012. Proton Users Revolt After CEO Calls Security Caution 'Loser Thinking' Amid AI Push

Proton Users Revolt After CEO Calls Security Caution 'Loser Thinking' Amid AI Push

Proton users are reporting subscription cancellations after revelations that CEO Andy Yen pressured engineers to embrace AI and dismissed a request for more security testing as “loser thinking.”

The backlash cuts into Proton’s main selling point—trusting it with information people want kept private. Its encrypted email service scrambles messages to protect their contents, and customers questioning its development practices are asking whether faster releases could come at their expense.

A Proton employee asked the company in August for a few days to pentest new code, meaning specialists would try to break into it before release, per 404 Media. Yen replied: “That’s bullshit and loser thinking. Of course it’s possible to be fast and secure.”

He also urged staff to stop writing code by hand and use more AI, and mentioned that he’d be reinterviewing its staff to see if and how they are using AI to do their job, the outlet reported. Its investigation drew on nine current and former employees, internal messages, and meeting recordings.

This didn’t land well among users, and customers have responded with cancellation notices and demands for new leadership on social media such as Reddit. A proposal on Proton’s feedback forum calling for Yen to step down had 2,020 votes when reviewed, alongside comments from users saying they had canceled subscriptions or would not renew.

Besides the blind use of AI, some former and current Proton employees on Reddit and social media have documented a toxic work culture, especially from the CEO.

Those posts document anger, rather than a verified count of departing customers. Canceling a paid plan also does not necessarily mean deleting an account.

Proton’s AI ambitions predate this dispute. Its Lumo chatbot comes with protections against using conversations to train AI, but internal use of AI was not among Proton’s selling points, even in the peak of the chatbot hype.

To address concerns over irresponsible use of AI, on November 1, technology chief Bart Butler published an engineering manifesto arguing that developers should direct large language models, AI systems that generate text and code, rather than treat them as occasional assistants.

Butler compared manual coding to memorizing phone numbers, and envisioned eventually ending most human code review. He also insisted that expertise and testing remain essential, with engineers accountable for work that models cannot reliably handle.

“Your job has fundamentally changed. It is no longer to write code, and LLMs are not here to help you write better code faster,” the manifesto reads. “Your job is to help the LLM write better code faster.” Doubling on its stance, Proton sees avoiding AI as an “existential threat” to the success of its very mission.

“‘Programming’ is dead. Engineering is not,” Buttler told his own team. “Your old job is gone”.

The document also calls for company-wide investment in automation, arguing that generating code faster achieves little if approvals and other development steps remain slow. Butler described that overhaul as the next phase of adoption.

AI tools can introduce security problems of their own. For example, Microsoft researchers said a now patched Claude flaw could expose secret access credentials through malicious instructions hidden in material the coding assistant reviewed.

They can also help find weaknesses: Google’s PageBreak system searches its applications for exploitable bugs and checks whether attacks actually work.

Proton acknowledged to 404 Media that management had sometimes communicated too bluntly and that employees were anxious. Its public manifesto nevertheless argues that failing to adopt AI quickly threatens the company’s ability to compete.

Yen canceled the re-interviews on October 30. The assessments would have required staff to explain their AI use, with employees fearing their answers could determine whether they kept their jobs.

#5010. Consensys launches cross-ledger settlement and routing stack for institutional networks

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Sovereign Network Interoperability stack supports securities settlement, cross-currency payments, and collateral mobility while allowing institutions to retain control of their ledgers.

Consensys on Monday announced a new technology stack designed to let banks and financial institutions settle transactions across independently operated blockchains without moving assets off their native ledgers.

Dubbed "Sovereign Network Interoperability," the product was launched at Devcon in Mumbai. It supports collateral mobility, securities settlement, wholesale and cross-currency payments, as well as  funds and asset servicing, according to a statement.

Consensys said the software stack includes a Cross Ledger Interoperability Protocol, built on a decade of research into zero-knowledge proofs. This architecture ensures bridgeless interoperability, meaning assets stay on their native ledgers while cross-ledger state and execution are verified cryptographically, it added.

Under the system, an institution specifies its transaction requirements and approved counterparties. The software selects an eligible route, coordinates execution on the participating ledgers and tracks the outcome.

"We are bringing deep protocol expertise, transaction coordination and complete support into one product," Consensys President David Cunningham said in the statement. "The result is a simpler path to each new connection, with verifiable outcomes and clear accountability for keeping the full stack working for years to come."

The product will also support the Ethereum Economic Zone standard, the Inter-Blockchain Communication protocol, and other interoperability technologies, per the statement. EVM-based networks are available now, while Canton connectivity is planned.

The launch follows Consensys' Oct. 8 partnership with ClearToken to develop infrastructure for round-the-clock settlement of tokenized securities and cash. In September, the company also announced plans to separate its consumer and institutional businesses, with MetaMask becoming an independent consumer-focused company and Consensys retaining its protocols and institutional infrastructure operations.

#5009. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, North Korea denied involvement in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin faced questions over continued wallet transfers following a $46 million security breach and Binance disclosed that it had tapped roughly half the Bitcoin in its publicly disclosed SAFU wallet as it worked to reimburse users affected by last week’s attack.

Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, denied North Korea’s involvement in the $6.3 billion Binance hack and appeared to suggest, without evidence, that the US could have been behind the theft.

In a statement released via the Korean Central News Agency (KCNA) on Tuesday, Kim rejected the FBI’s attribution of the attack to a North Korean-backed hacking group.

“The FBI is trying to brand the DPRK as a ‘cyber criminal state’, claiming that it obtained information that a hacking organization ‘supported’ by the DPRK was involved in the incident,” said Kim. “But the US assertion is an absurd rhetoric and nothing new.”

She described the allegations as a political provocation intended to damage North Korea’s international standing. The FBI also blamed North Korea for the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, an accusation Kim also rejected on Tuesday.

“This is not the first time that the U.S. has attempted to spread misunderstanding of the DPRK in the international community, blindly linking the DPRK with the ‘cyber crime’ incidents whenever they occur on an international scale,” said Kim.

Mercado Bitcoin, one of Brazil’s largest cryptocurrency exchanges, confirmed a security incident on Monday that blockchain analysts estimated drained more than $46 million from its wallets. Despite the company’s announcement that it had suspended deposits and withdrawals, addresses associated with the incident continued to receive funds for hours afterward.

Senior security engineer at Certik, Dickson Wu, published an initial post-mortem of the incident, saying the attackers “picked at eight small chains for four hours,” including Base, Optimism and Arbitrum. The attack then moved to the company’s main hot wallets before transferring tiny amounts of Ether (ETH) from 27,160 different addresses.

“So whoever did this can also move money out of MB’s customer deposit addresses,” he wrote.

Security firms have stated the hack was carried out through standard transfers rather than a smart contract exploit.

A spokesperson for Mercado Bitcoin told Cointelegraph that customers had suffered no losses and no data had been leaked, but the company did not respond to requests for comment on the continued transfers. A data breach could also trigger notification requirements to Brazil’s data protection authority.

The hack has not yet been attributed to an organized group and remains under investigation.

Binance’s publicly disclosed emergency Bitcoin wallet held roughly half its previous balance on Monday after the exchange tapped its user protection fund to cover losses from Thursday’s $6.3 billion hack.

The exchange confirmed a transfer of 7,499 Bitcoin (BTC) on Saturday, linking it to the partial deployment of its Secure Asset Fund for Users (SAFU).

As of Monday, the wallet held about 7,500 BTC, worth $583 million, down from about 15,000 BTC before the transfer.

Binance began reopening withdrawals in phases on Sunday and said it had replenished approximately 95% of the affected assets, up from 90% a day earlier. It continued buying the remaining, less-liquid tokens through market and over-the-counter transactions.

The exchange is using its corporate treasury, SAFU and part of its futures insurance fund to cover the losses.

#5008. The Best Dining Room Lighting Ideas to Set Your Space Aglow

As romantic as candlelight dining sounds, it’s simply not practical (way too much wax!). The best dining room lighting ideas are thoughtfully planned: They take your actual eating habits, furniture proportions, room structure, and budget into consideration. They weigh the benefits of pendant lights vs. chandeliers vs. flush mounts and wall sconces, not to mention bulb type.

Lighting, it turns out, gets pretty involved—which is why we’ve tapped interior designers from around the country for their brightest ideas. And though the considerations are complex, modern lighting can be just as romantic as the candlelight option. “The evolving landscape is opening the door to more thoughtful layers and innovative ideas for how lighting can shape a space,” says Mike Shively of Chicago-based AD PRO firm En Masse Architecture and Design. “Lighting and coziness are both things we associate with luxury today, and neither necessarily comes down to price.”

In fashion designer Marysia Reeves’s Venice Beach home, a breezy, lantern-like fixture hovers above the wood dining table.

Pendant lighting gets more playful when each piece hangs at a different height. The look works especially well with a vaulted ceiling, as evidenced by this dining room set in a pavilion at the back of the house. “A cluster of glass pendants hung at staggered heights works alongside a pair of table lamps on the sideboard to create warm, layered light,” says Lucas Goldbach of En Masse Architecture and Design, who worked on the project.

The varying heights of the pendants gives this light fixture some dimension.

In this stunner of an LA home by design collective the Archers, a Le Corbusier–inspired table is lit with simple can overhead lights (definitely dimmable versions).

Lighting options are about more than just form. A more traditional shape can look entirely new when cast in a surprising material. “Our recommendation is to rethink conventional finishes and materials wherever possible,” says Sarah Goesling of Goesling Group, an AD PRO Directory firm based in Chicago. “For a chain-mail fixture, look beyond stainless steel to a striking yellow gold. For braided metal hardware, trade the ruggedness of wrought iron for the refined sheen of polished gunmetal.”

For a dining room in a midcentury-modern home, Goesling Group chose an appropriately retro 1950s-inspired Nabila chandelier by Tooy. And to add a modernized touch through the materials, they went with smoked glass around the bulbs.

A Sputnik-style fixture draws the eye up in this dining room by Goesling Group.

If you find an antique fixture you love, it might be worth rewiring the piece. Source on sites like eBay, Etsy, and 1stdibs, or take a lap around your local flea market. Alternatively, scour relatives’ attics and basements for a fixture with some family history. In the dining room below, designed by En Masse Architecture and Design, the light fixture was originally bought by the homeowner’s parents and was refurbished by Archistoric. “It became the inspiration for many of the metal finishes used throughout the new design,” says Goldbach.

This En Masse Architecture and Design dining space is crowned by an heirloom chandelier, refurbished by Archistoric.

Sconces, floor lamps, even table lamps can do the job of overhead pendant and flush-mount fixtures—so don’t force one if you’re not sure you like the look at all (proof, via Amélie Colombet’s apartment).

Fixtures made from natural alabaster stone are popular because of the warm, glare-free glow they emit. And because the translucent material has natural veining, no two pieces are alike. You can find alabaster in a variety of shapes—including linear forms, mini pendants, and semi-flush-mount ceiling lights—and while it’s generally pretty pricey (ranging anywhere from $300 for a single alabaster pendant lamp to $3,000 for a row of pendants), it’s also a durable material that lasts.

In the dining nook below, designed by Goesling Group, the minimal fixture makes a major impression. “It’s a solid alabaster stone piece lit all the way around the rim that looks like the moon when it glows,” says Goesling.

The pendant sets a striking tone in this cozy dining nook by Goesling Group.

If you have no interest in hardwiring a thing . . .Get an arc floor lampYou can have proper overhead lighting for your dining area without hardwiring a thing overhead. All you need is an arc floor lamp; they plug right into a wall outlet but stand tall, with a long, curved neck that will position the bulb and shade directly over your table. (How’s that for simplified?) The Arco floor lamp by Flos is a classic, but more budget-conscious shoppers can spring for similar styles from retailers like Wayfair (or even Amazon!).

If you’re trying to decide between options . . .Take the shape and style of your table into considerationThe overhead fixture you choose and the table itself “don’t have to [have] a direct geometrical correlation, but there should be some proportional consideration,” says Russell Groves of New York City–based Groves & Co. A long or unusually large chandelier would obviously overpower a short table, and vice versa: A teeny-tiny bulb might look silly above a big table. Same goes for the look: A lovely old farmhouse table would do well with a lovely farmhouse fixture or something super-modern for contrast—but either way, they should be considered together.

If you’d like to jazz up a fixture you already own . . .Customize the lampshadeA reflective material inside the shade will help cast light back down toward the table, Groves points out, while perforated shades will turn any bulb into a twinkly light (if that’s your prerogative). “Textured fabrics like raw silk will add a beautiful warmth and shadow to a room,” he says, but should you require a more directional cast, “an opaque shade wouldn’t give you a lot of glow in the room but would put more on the table.” Just because a fixture has shades you’re not into doesn’t mean it’s the end of the line. Swap them out!

If you’re sick of the typical dining room look . . .Consider less-traditional fixtures“Unreal times call for surreal styles, and lighting is emerging as a particularly compelling canvas for experimentation,” says Goesling. “From blown-glass designs with off-kilter stripes, to chain-mail, tapestry, and wrought iron reimagined through unexpected finishes and geometric patterns, these materials and techniques are taking on a new life. We anticipate seeing this influence extend across lighting, from woven metal sheaths and illustrative shades to sculptural, cast forms.”

A unique accent light sometimes even doubles as art. Such is the case in a Chicago penthouse designed by Kuchar, which features a dining room fixture with a strikingly sinuous form. “Going for the cool, funky light fixture has become the new normal among our residential clients,” says Sarah Kuchar-Parkinson, owner and creative director of the Chicago-based studio. “Not only does it bring brightness and light, but it adds architectural interest and an element of quirk.”

A statement-making light fixture hovers above the dining room table of this Kuchar-designed Chicago penthouse.

When hanging a pendant or a chandelier . . .Take care to hang it at the correct heightToo high and a pendant lamp might look stuck to the ceiling, constricted; too low and you won’t be able to see your guests around it. Somewhere in between is just right, so step back and consider it at various lengths before settling on one. “When you’re seated, you don’t want [the fixture] to be in your face—the same way everyone gets annoyed when flowers are in the way,” Groves says.

If you’re into geometric lines . . .Get creative with the cordWhen hanging a pendant lamp with a very simple silhouette, feel free to get creative with the cord: Zigzag it from wall to wall around a corner, tack it in swags along the ceiling, or loop it over a hook on the wall.

To soften overly bright overheads . . .Add a dimmerDesigner Sam Allen of Sam Allen Interiors, based in Weston, Connecticut, feels strongly about the worth of a dimmer switch: “If your dining room chandelier is not hooked up to a dimmer, call an electrician right now. I mean it. Don’t even finish reading this—call your electrician first.” With the simple pressing of a lever or turning of a knob, dimmable lighting allows you to control the brightness of the bulbs, which is particularly important if you’re dealing with often-harsh overhead lighting. “To me, chandeliers should mimic candlelight,” says Groves—and that warm, dim glow is possible if you just install dimmers. Plus, they’re available in both modern and traditional silhouettes that will add considerably more charm to the walls than those plastic switch plates you hate anyway.

To make the room multifunctional . . .Supplement the lighting solution you chooseIf you end up setting your table with some nice, warm candlelight, you might need to add sconces or downlights to supplement that glow when the room is used for anything other than an intimate dinner. (Dining room tables are very good for DIY projects, we’d like to note.) And to up a room’s cozy factor, lean into lamps. “The more lamps the better,” says Goldbach. “We like using smaller-scale lamps and vintage pieces in unexpected ways to create a warm, layered atmosphere. Multiple light sources can make the space feel cozier and more inviting, especially as the days get shorter.”

If you’re a fanatic about light quality . . .Be selective with the bulbsWarm white light, cool light, bright light—you can get exactly the brightness level you desire with the right bulb. For lots of ambience, try vintage-inspired filament bulbs. If it’s more of a particular shape and style you’re after for an exposed-bulb fixture, try decorative bulbs. And for the ultimate in energy efficiency, invest in LED bulbs, which are up to 90 percent more efficient than traditional light bulbs.

#5006. North Korea Is Laundering Binance Hack Loot at One New Wallet Every 25 Seconds

North Korea Is Laundering Binance Hack Loot at One New Wallet Every 25 Seconds

North Korean hackers are moving the $6.4 billion they stole from Binance at a blistering clip: roughly one new crypto wallet every 25 seconds.

In under four days, the attacker has spread the funds across about 12,000 wallets on 11 blockchains in more than 40,000 transactions, according to an analysis published Monday by blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

But stablecoin issuers moved even faster. About $2.85 billion, or 44% of the haul, has been frozen or seized, compared with roughly 3% after North Korea’s $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025.

Speed mattered because stablecoins made up $3.46 billion, or 54%, of the haul, and their issuers can freeze them. Tether froze the stolen USDT on Ethereum 37 minutes after it reached the attacker’s wallet, and Circle froze all $823 million in stolen USDC before any of it could be swapped, Elliptic found.

Before the freezes landed, the hacker swapped 434 million USDT for ETH on decentralized exchanges such as Uniswap, borrowed 19,762 ETH on lending protocol Aave against another 59.9 million USDT, and traded 234 million USDT for Tron’s TRX token. In all, about $786 million in stablecoins got away.

Attackers began draining Binance’s hot wallets late Thursday. The FBI has since attributed the theft to TraderTraitor, a North Korean state-linked hacking group, and published hundreds of linked addresses.

Binance, which has pledged to cover all losses, reopened withdrawals Sunday and saw about $3.64 billion in net outflows in the first nine and a half hours.

The laundering that followed has been relentless. On Tron, which became the main route, activity peaked at about 1,300 transactions in a single hour.

That pace fueled speculation that AI agents were doing the work. Elliptic disagreed, pointing to fixed timing, set amounts, blind retries and pauses that resemble human working hours. The firm wrote that while the laundering is clearly automated, “nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions.”

About $3.24 billion remains in addresses linked to the theft, mostly ETH and the 18,270 stolen Bitcoin, which haven’t moved since the day of the attack. Elliptic believes those funds are very likely still held by North Korea, while money already being laundered may have passed to Chinese money-laundering networks.

Only a negligible amount appears to have been cashed out. About $16 million has also flowed to addresses holding proceeds of September’s Bitget exploit.

Based on the Bybit playbook, Elliptic expects the attacker to swap its ETH for Bitcoin in small batches and cash out through over-the-counter brokers over a period of months. Further freezes will be harder, the firm said, since ETH and Bitcoin can’t be frozen. The best remaining chances lie with centralized services, where the funds will eventually have to surface.

The Binance theft caps a punishing stretch for crypto security. BTCPay Server urged merchants to switch off their servers amid reports of hot wallet attacks on Monday, and Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin halted withdrawals after a $40 million hack. It’s unclear whether either incident is connected to the Binance attack.

#5005. NEAR governance approves proposal to cut token issuance ceiling to 1.6%

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

NEAR's House of Stake passed HSP-029, which lowers the network's maximum annual issuance rate from 2.5% to 1.6% over 24 months.

NEAR Protocol's House of Stake governance body has approved a proposal to lower the network's maximum annual issuance rate from 2.5% to 1.6% over 24 months.

HSP-029, which passed the ballot on Monday with overwhelming support, retains the existing 90/10 split of newly issued tokens between staking participants and the protocol treasury, and changes only that single protocol parameter. This would not alter the emissions mechanism.

Once implemented, the issuance ceiling declines each epoch linearly at roughly 3.75 basis points per month, reaching the 1.6% target at the 24th month. This is essentially to slow dilution and reduce long-term sell pressure of the NEAR token NEAR Protocol (NEAR), as the network said its bootstrapping phase is largely over.

"This is how NEAR's economics evolve transparently and programmatically: token holders put a core parameter to a public vote, and the new rate rolls out epoch by epoch on a published schedule," NEAR said in an X post. "For NEAR's tokenomics, that means lower long-term issuance and a supply schedule anyone can verify."

The proposal estimates the ramp would avoid roughly 11.8 million NEAR of issuance over the first two years and 66.1 million over six years, leaving the supply about 4.36% smaller than under the existing 2.5% ceiling. Daily issuance would fall from approximately 89,500 NEAR to 57,300 NEAR at full implementation, while gross staking yield is projected to decline from 5.268% to 3.372% over the same period, assuming the staking participation rate measured in September remains unchanged.

For the proposal to go into full effect, the House of Stake Security Council will need to review the result before ratification. A 90-day grace period follows, after which validators must adopt a protocol upgrade for the change to become binding. NEAR previously cut the maximum issuance from 5% to 2.5% in October 2025.

#5004. BlackRock’s IBIT sees biggest Bitcoin ETF outflows since June

BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF shed $405.3 million on Monday as US spot Bitcoin funds opened November with nearly $399 million in net outflows.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded its largest daily outflow since June on Monday as US-listed spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) kicked off November with further withdrawals.

The funds recorded $398.6 million in net outflows, led by $405.3 million from IBIT, according to Farside Investors data.

IBIT’s outflow was its largest since June 26, when investors pulled $444.5 million. Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) attracted $6.7 million on Monday, partially offsetting the outflows.

The withdrawals followed $515 million in net outflows from US spot Bitcoin ETFs in October. The funds have recorded around $38 million in net outflows so far in 2026.

“I wouldn’t interpret these outflows as a rejection of digital assets,” Edwin Mata, CEO of tokenization company Brickken, said in commentary published Monday. “Investors reduce exposure when uncertainty rises, particularly after an event that raises questions about the security of market infrastructure.”

The withdrawals came amid broader crypto market volatility following the $6.3 billion Binance hack.

Bitcoin briefly fell below $76,400 on Monday, its lowest level since August, according to CoinGecko. The cryptocurrency traded at around $76,600 at publication, down 3.7% over the past seven days.

US spot Ether (ETH) ETFs also recorded about $140 million in net outflows on Monday, extending their losing streak to six trading days after posting a record $1.61 billion in monthly outflows in October, according to SoSoValue data.

#5001. 12 Best Historic Hotels and Airbnbs That Feel Like Time Traveling (2026)

There are plenty of impressive stays out there, no matter your destination of choice. But booking a historic hotel or Airbnb rental with a storied past—tales of tenants, centuries-old architecture, or whatever else might land a property on a local registrar—is the difference between a simple spot to snooze at night and your next dinner party conversation. From former artists’ homes and firehouses to castles and celebrity-studded hotels, we’ve curated several beguiling places around the world to lay your head at night. As is the case with older buildings, some require a bit of flexibility—say, an outdoor shower or a less-than-fully-equipped kitchen—while others offer a portal to the past (some spots on this list predate the 16th century) rich with amenities and luxury.

The following historical hotels and homes have a story to tell, and guests get a front-row seat.

Perks: Shared beach access, in-unit washer and dryer, sound system, indoor fireplace, fire pit

Architect Frank Lloyd Wright built a lot of incredible spaces for others, but this is the very one he contributed to a Life Magazine article back in 1938 as part of a feature about dream homes. The four-bedroom estate is full of windows (the 63-foot living room has an entire wall of them), and each space is bursting with midcentury furniture and decor. Walking into each room feels like unlocking a new level of a time capsule, from the shag-carpeted primary bedroom to the brick-walled kitchen. While spring, summer, and fall are beautiful times to visit Wisconsin, the fire pit (and hammock) outside is still inviting come winter and snow. From $841 per night.

Perks: Badminton net, steam room, gym, BBQ grill, EV charger

This is the perfect pit stop on a road trip through the Highlands. Hidden in the depths of a former abbey, this one-bedroom space used to be the “writing room” for the monastery. The bed is surrounded by stained glass windows, which look out over the gardens and Loch Ness (yes, of Nessie fame). Guests have access to the neighboring Highland Club, which houses an incredible indoor pool, sauna, tennis court, and so much more. While the kitchen and appliances are newer additions, you will find pieces of furniture from the original space peppered throughout the rental (a wooden bench here, a tapestry there). From $468 per night.

Perks: Pet friendly, patio, free parking, pool, outdoor shower

If daydreams of your next trip are filled with al fresco dinners, this two-bedroom home will do you one better by adding bird’s-eye ocean vistas to the scene. Once a windmill that used to grind wheat for flour production, it now houses an outdoor kitchen, a bedroom with a view, and an outdoor shower. The scenes from the top bedroom are really something to write home about—wake up to mountain views and the ocean beyond. The pool is the best spot to take in the sunset before retreating to the open-air dining space for good eats and conversation. From $224 per night.

Perks: Pet friendly, Wi-Fi, luggage drop-off, radiant heating, fireplace

This historic Airbnb is known as La Romantica, which should tell you all you need to know. The one-bedroom rental can be found in the oldest section of the property (which dates back earlier than the 1400s), there’s a private entrance, ancient stone walls, antique Italian furniture, and a fireplace that’s perfect for fighting off a chilly night. This is a spot where you do a bit of time traveling, both indoors and out when you decide to explore the surrounding UNESCO heritage town, Teggiano, which is known for its museums (the Diocesan Museum, Herb Museum, and Museum of Folk Traditions, to name a few) and its 13 churches. Guests at this one-bedroom stay are located right in the middle of town and within walking distance of everything you’d need, so slipping out for a quick dinner, coffee at one of the cafes, or a sunset stroll along medieval architecture is always on the itinerary. From $154 per night.

Perks: Pet friendly, air conditioning, private parking available, fireplace, historic decor

Built in 1884, this was Washington, DC’s first fully African American fire company and the set of a Netflix show: Check out Stay Here, season one, episode eight. Located in the Shaw neighborhood, a historic district, there are plays to attend (Howard Theatre), drinks to be had (Blagden Alley), and music to take in (Eighth Street Lounge and Bar). The five-bedroom rental even has the original fire pole and you can see previous tenants in the photography that decorates the walls. From $860 per night.

Perks: Shared beach access, sound system, exercise equipment, fire pit

Step right into the world of painter Reginald Marsh when you stay at this two-bedroom getaway with forest views. A pond anchors one side of the property, and the expansive lawns are landscaped with long, post-dinner strolls in mind. As secluded as it feels, it’s a short walk to the center of Woodstock and all of its restaurants, bars, cafes, and shops. And while you’re not near any national parks, the home’s private waterfall (you read that correctly) borders a public preserve that’s open for exploring. The library inside will pull you back indoors, though, as will the wood-burning stove on chilly nights. The outdoor dining space is great for summertime grilling, but the full kitchen indoors is a delight to work in. From $619 per night.

Perks: Central location, trendy bars and restaurant, 24-hour fitness center, balcony access, Aqua di Parma and Byredo toiletries

Centrally located in Madrid’s Plaza de las Cortes, the historic Palace Hotel reopened its doors in 2025 to unveil property-wide updates—including its main lobby, dining accommodations, and 470 rooms—the result of a two-year renovation project with reimagined interiors by designer Lázaro Rosa-Violán. Dating back to 1912, The Palace has seen notable guests like Salvador Dalí and Ernest Hemingway, and is even credited as the first hotel in the country to have private toilets in its quarters.

Today, guests can appreciate pieces of the property’s historic past through a modern lens. Surrounded by bespoke fixtures custom designed by Rosa-Violán, vintage tapestries hang with contemporary Joan Miro prints in La Cúpula Restaurant. Inside the inviting suites, updated bathrooms feature original red marble stone reminiscent of Paloma Elsesser’s luxurious tub. Just a five-minute walk away, art aficionados can spend long hours wandering through the Thyssen-Bornemisza National Museum and the Prado museum, or take a stroll through El Retiro Park. “The hotel staff at The Palace are some of the friendliest I’ve encountered,” says commerce editor Audrey Lee. “You’ll definitely want to stop by La Cúpula during your trip for an afternoon drink and some bites from the stylish tapas menu. If it’s the right time of day, the room becomes drenched in sunlight through the dome-shaped stained glass ceiling.” From $1,060 per night.

Perks: Spa, fitness center, concierge, Smart TVs, room service

The history of the Waldorf Astoria runs deep. Originally designed in 1893 as two side-by-side hotels built by feuding family members, the collective property was demolished in 1929 to make room for the Empire State Building’s construction. It was reopened in its current Park Avenue location in 1931, breaking records as the largest and tallest hotel in the world (at the time). Its Art Deco architecture and interiors caught the eyes of royals, celebrities, and politicians around the world. The guest book includes names such as Nikola Tesla, Winston Churchill, Zsa Zsa Gabor, the Obamas, and so many more. After being closed for extensive renovations, the property is planning a grand re-opening for Spring 2025. From $1,221 per night.

Perks: Irene Forte spa, indoor lap pool, three on-site restaurants, whisky tasting experience, afternoon tea, views of Edinburgh landmarks

A historic landmark, the Balmoral is widely recognized for its 190-foot clock tower—famously set three minutes fast, except on New Year’s Eve—that has been part of the Edinburgh skyline since opening in the early 1900s. J.K. Rowling even finished the final Harry Potter book on January 11, 2007, in Suite 552—now commemorated with an owl door knocker and an in-room bust of the Greek god Hermes that Rowling signed. “The Balmoral does rest and relaxation like no other through comforting amenities, charming interiors, and hearty cuisine,” says shopping editor Audrey Lee. “After a long day of taking in Edinburgh’s historic sights, there was nothing I looked forward to more than the treats from turndown service and a long soak in the glossy marble tub.” Guests also return for the sprawling bedrooms, some of which frame picturesque views of Edinburgh Castle. The stays showcase cozy design with contemporary wallpaper prints, surprisingly neutral plaid furniture, and ultrasoft wool bed covers. From $399 per night.

Perks: Presidential, royal, and celebrity clientele; iconic Bemelmans Bar; walking distance to Central Park

Nestled on 76th Street off Madison Avenue, the Carlyle first opened its doors in 1930. Since then, presidents, royalty, and movie stars have all sought refuge in the hallowed hotel. “You can have breakfast next to Daniel Patrick Moynihan or Tom Ford,” Los Angeles–based designer and AD100 Hall of Famer Michael S. Smith once described. “It's like a big hotel in a small town, the grande dame of New York.” The interiors blend Art Deco touches and New York eclecticism with classic architecture through a layered mix of vintage-inspired furniture silhouettes, contemporary art, and splashy chandeliers. One of the hotel’s top attractions for locals and hotel guests alike is the iconic Bemelmans Bar, outfitted with whimsical wall murals by Madeline author and illustrator Ludwig Bemelmans. The classic martini is a failsafe order, though you could go for the namesake Madeline's Vesper or Bobby’s Manhattan (an homage to cabaret singer Bobby Short, a longtime fixture of Café Carlyle, another classic destination within the Carlyle). From $1,323 per night.

Celebrating its 100th birthday this year, Hotel Figueroa was founded in partnership with The Young Women's Christian Association (YWCA) as a safe haven for solo female travelers. The Downtown Los Angeles fixture was the first property that allowed unaccompanied women to check in without a male chaperone—something most hotels prohibited at the time. Today, Spanish Revival design influences flourish in the space: Expect curated art (exclusively by female artists), sophisticated tilework, and modern furnishings. As you dip in the coffin-shaped pool straight from the 1920s while surrounded by 50-year-old cacti, enjoy craft cocktails and bites from the poolside bar, La Casita. For a more substantial meal, the restaurant-in-residence Florence by the Water serves up Italian classics. From $229 per night.

Perks: Proximity to landmarks, celebrity guest roster, traditional afternoon tea, live music, private tours and experiences available upon request

Widely recognized as London’s oldest luxury hotel, updates like glossy marble bathrooms, vibrant textiles, and mahogany walk-in closets have breathed new life into the historic stay. At the same time, homages to the original architecture—in stained-glass windows, untouched entryways, and wrought-iron façade detailing—forge bonds to its heritage. “You might think the main attraction of Brown’s is its proximity to landmarks like Buckingham Palace and Hyde Park, but it’s actually the inviting staff and dapper design that make this stay more like a home than a stopover,” says Lee. Past literary guests of note include Rudyard Kipling, Bram Stoker, Agatha Christie, and Stephen King; King even outlined his bestseller Misery during one of his stays. Situated in the upscale Mayfair neighborhood, world-class shopping and top London museums ensure your agenda stays full. From $966 per night.

Craving an escape? From colorful carry-ons to cleverly designed packing cubes (how did we ever travel without them?), shop everything you need to make the journey in style—and as streamlined and stress-free as possible.
